## Global Data Watch

![](images/b1592267e97e2aac86d24261821f1c784b4e7db5a65459b0c3b59f1b5132e992.jpg)

Both growth and inflation shifting toward goods around midyear

• Overall growth is downshifting but not threatening weakness

0 China demand weakness is not holding back the rest of Asia

• Next week: EA Flash PMI rebounds; 2% 2Q US GDP; BoC on hold

## Orderly rotation

Incoming news remains supportive of our view that the expansion is building a broader base even as global GDP growth moderates. The most notable recent news comes from the beleaguered manufacturing sector where a 0.6% output rise in May, was followed by upbeat US and China June reports this week. Global manufacturing appears to be rising at a faster than 2%ar at midyear and solid final demand growth looks set to sustain gains at this pace. Consumer goods spending (outside China) is rising modestly while Asian tech activity is rebounding from an early year pause. We forecast a service sector cooling to lower global growth to a trend-like pace as we move through midyear (Figure 1). Readings from labor markets and our nowcasters are consistent with a downshift but point to upside risk to our forecast for global GDP gains at a 2%ar in the middle quarters of the year.

Less clear cut is the degree to which an expected regional rotation in demand away from the US is progressing. Next week’s US GDP report is expected to confirm that 1H24 growth cooled to below a 2%ar. However, US domestic demand is tracking close to a 3%ar 1H24 increase as a large net trade drag provides a substantial global impulse. Solid growth in EM ex. China complements the US, but this week’s reports point to weakness in China domestic demand and Euro area industrial activity. An expected July Euro area flash PMI rebound next week should help calm concern that Western Europe is slipping below our forecast for 1% 2H24 growth.

The fading of the 2022-23 monetary tightening drag—evident in this week’s Euro area bank lending survey—and easing guidance from a number of DM central banks reinforce the case for breadth and resilience. At this week’s ECB meeting President Lagarde said that the September decision is “wide open” and that there is no precommitment to a particular rate path. But by reconfirming the June inflation outlook, the Governing Council has implicitly endorsed the September rate cut built into that

baseline. In the UK, firm growth and inflation readings keep open debate, but the minutes of the June MPC meeting guide toward an August cut. Although the Fed is signaling that it is not ready to ease this month, two consecutive inflation surprises have built enough confidence that it is now guiding strongly toward a September easing (Figure 2).

Consistent with the growth rotation now underway the drivers of global goods and service prices are pointing in different directions. Global goods price pressures appear to be building, with a sharp rise in global transportation costs combining with a firming in pricing power as manufacturing picks up. At the same time, global service price inflation is moderating at midyear. This deceleration largely reflects the unwind of concentrated early year price increases. However, underlying fundamentals also point to disinflation ahead as service sector demand cools and wage inflation moderates. The net effect of this rotation will be to push global core CPI inflation lower in 2H24. Disinflation in service prices is, however, expected to remain incomplete as labor costs continue to rise at an elevated pace and the level of global service sector activity remains high relative to its pre-pandemic trend. As such, we expect global core inflation to remain close to a 3%ar in 2H24.

## A risk bias and a correlated surprise lever

As the start of a broad DM easing cycle approaches, rates markets are increasing expectations of policy action for the coming year. The path for both the US and Western Europe now incorporates a faster than 25bp per quarter pace through end-2025. Embedded in that path are the risks associated with US policy changes following the upcoming election. The potential for fiscal policy shifts is an important risk but changes on this front would likely not materialize until 2026. The more immediate risk is that global trade tension increases. A trade conflict that increases tariffs and limits activity could both raise inflation and hurt growth. But markets recognize that the bias of risk is skewed in the direction of lower rates. There are two sources to this bias. First, rising inflation related to tariff increases is a negative supply shock. In an environment in which growth is slowing, central banks are not likely to see higher inflation as persistent. Second, rising concern about both weak growth and high inflation should, on balance, bias central bankers to shift stances toward neutral. With policy stances currently perceived as restrictive, it is thus more likely for central banks to ease.

Alongside this bias in central bank reaction functions to widening uncertainty, we also emphasize the leverage of realized action to correlated macroeconomic surprises. This point comes across in considering the underpinnings of the Fed dot plot of aggressive easing from the lens of a balanced Taylor rule (Table 1). Based on their views on inflation, slack, and neutral policy rates, a roughly 200bp policy easing would be warranted. But just as a slide toward recession would likely lever easing, a combination of modest upside surprises to wage and price inflation and a renewed tightening in labor markets would sharply curtail rate cuts, particularly as it influences central bankers’ assessments of risk and the unobservable estimates of neutral rates and slack.

## Expecting Bank of Canada to hold

The Bank of Canada meets next week, and the decision looks to be a very close call. The case for patience starts with Governor Macklem, who previously advocated a “gradual” easing cycle with concern that cutting too rapidly “could jeopardize the progress we’ve made” on inflation. Since the June rate cut, the 3-month annualized growth of the BoC’s preferred core inflation measures has reaccelerated to the top of its target band. Businesses also were more uncertain about the inflation outlook in 2Q while wages have firmed recently against some further labor market softening. The BoC may not want to surprise markets that are nearly fully priced for a 25bp cut this month, but we think the economic evidence— alongside a gradual Fed—makes a more compelling case to wait until September.

## Consumers perking up unevenly

Consumer spending looks to be improving after a soft patch in retail sales earlier this year raised some concern that the expansion was losing more steam than expected. Even excluding China, where consumers continue to be a material drag, DM retail sales slumped in 1Q24 alongside a particularly large drop in motor vehicle sales (Figure 3). Overall consumption expenditures in the DM decelerated as well to a modest 1%ar, but were cushioned by more robust service sector spending. We have been inclined to fade the weakness in consumer goods spending through 1H24 given the strength of the fundamentals. In a note this week, we highlight the strength of job growth and wage inflation that is sustaining DM labor income at the fastest pace of growth seen in

decades. Combined with ongoing wealth gains, it is hard to not see the consumer adding upside risk to our modest growth outlook in 2H24.

![](images/3aa403761ac1ccbd61df9862be28dbc489f1215dd2c56227caf0079da6405b0f.jpg)

## China hobbles into midyear

Following the 1Q rebound, China’s GDP decelerated sharply to a weak 1%ar last quarter. The economy remains extremely imbalanced, with robust manufacturing investment and exports being offset by weak consumer spending and a contracting real estate sector. Consumer demand has yet to find a firm footing this year, as retail sales only rose 3.7%oya in 1H (with a large 1.3% decline in June). On the production side, manufacturing output posted a strong 1.1% gain last month, but this is geared toward exports (up nearly 14%oya from January to May) and inventories. With overcapacity in production and a likely inventory overhang, price competition is intense—potentially softening our call for global goods prices to strengthen in 2H24. In addition, on the fiscal policy side, the pace of special local government bond issuance is notably behind normal years (only 38% of full-year LG bond quota has been issued in 1H, vs. 71% in normal years). As such, infra-FAI only showed rather moderate growth at 5.4%oya in 1H. The policy response remains quiet. This week’s Third Plenum came in line with expectations and delivered no major surprises. The next opportunity to boost policy support will come later this month during the Politburo meeting.

## Upsides being realized in Asia

As we have been flagging in recent weeks, the strength of external demand has created meaningful upside growth risks across EM Asia outside of Mainland China. We recently sharply marked up Taiwan’s 2Q GDP, as strong tech demand is also spilling over into the domestic economy. Similarly, this week Malaysia’s 2Q GDP came in much stronger than expected, with broad-based gains across sectors owing in part to recent demand-side policy measures. In Korea, we expect next week’s flash 2Q GDP to reflect a technical correction to outsized gains in 1Q, as robust tech exports are offset by softer domestic demand, particularly construction investment.

## India: Consolidation without pain

Markets are keenly awaiting the first budget of Prime Minister Modi’s third term next week. With the ruling party losing its absolute majority in the June parliamentary elections, there are concerns that authorities may turn fiscally expensive. We do not see this and expect the budget to continue on a path of consolidation, with the central deficit expected to be reduced 0.5%-pt to 5.1% of GDP this fiscal year. However, we do not expect this to be a painful adjustment given an unexpectedly large annual dividend from the RBI and stronger-than-expected tax revenues. As a result, authorities have about 0.5% of GDP in extra revenues that had not been envisaged, generated from foreign interest income and a revaluation of foreign currency reserves.

## Brazil announces spending freeze

Finance Minister Fernando Haddad announced a BRL15bn freeze in spending from this year’s budget to comply with its fiscal target. While the amount is likely insufficient to eliminate the primary deficit, we view the announcement as positive considering current political constraints. The decision comes against a backdrop of resilient activity that, despite the Rio Grande do Sul floods, has prompted a 0.3%-pt upgrade to our 2024 GDP forecast to 2.5%. Signaling a commitment to the fiscal target and a framework ahead of the August 31 budget proposal should ease concerns over the government’s ability to meet its fiscal goals, helping to stem a further sell-off in the currency and allow the BCB to keep rates on hold as we expect (markets leaning toward hikes).

## SARB: looking for a September cut

An improvement in South Africa’s near-term inflation outlook is raising the odds of an earlier start to the easing cycle. Amid weak growth, inflation is set to dip below the SARB’s target over the next three quarters, while political and fiscal risks have receded after President Ramaphosa formed a government of national unity without far-left political parties. The SARB has retained a restrictive policy stance (with the real rate about 100bp above neutral) and this week held rates at 8.25% amid lingering concerns over the currency and services inflation. However, with two members opting to ease, chances for a cut at the September meeting have risen. We now look for two cuts this year (in September and November) and 100bp of easing by mid-2025 (previously 75bp).

## Economic Activity Tracking

See here for a real-time snapshot and methodology reports.

Our estimate for 2Q24 global real GDP growth slipped 0.5%-pt this week to 1.8%ar (Figure 1), while the topdown global nowcaster for 2Q moved sideways at 2.4%ar. The nowcaster is tracking 3Q growth at 2.3%ar (Table 2).

A quarter of the economies we track have seen data surprise stronger over the past 30 days. The nowcaster implies upside risk to 2Q growth in a little over half of these economies (Figure 2).

Our CapexNow model suggests an acceleration in capex growth from 2.4%ar in 1Q24 to 3.0%ar in 2Q24 (Table 4).

May data show a bounce in our global good sector aggregates: global retail sales rose 0.7%, and manufacturing output gained 0.6%. We continue to see solid fundamentals in place to support a consumption-led industry lift in 2H24. However, malaise among G-4 consumers looks to have worsened in 2Q.

## J.P. Morgan Market Watch

## Bonds

Bonds were mixed this week. US yields were modestly higher, amid firmer activity data as well as a rise in expectations of a Trump victory in November. On labor markets, softer claims data pointed to gradual cooling, but could be affected by Hurricane Beryl. While the typical dynamics into a first ease would support lower yields and a steeper curve, markets are pricing in a more aggressive pace of cuts than we expect. As a result, we keep 5s/30s steepeners but added beta-weighted shorts in 2Y Treasuries as a hedge.

In the Euro area, the ECB stayed on hold without giving an explicit signal for September, in line with a more data dependent approach. Our economists continue to see a September cut. We hold tactical longs in 5Y Germany, steepeners in 2Yx2Y/10Yx10Y EUR swaps, and a bias for a steeper 5Yx30Y German curve. In the UK, we continue to see a 25bp cut by the BoE at the August meeting, but see it as a close call. We hold long Sep24 MPC OIS, 1Y/1Yx1Y SONIA curve flatteners and long 5Yx5Y SONIA vs. SOFR. In Japan, we expect the BoJ to hike policy rates and provide greater clarity over the QT pace at the July meeting. We hold 5s/30s JGB curve steepeners.

In EM, with US elections starting to dominate the news flow, polling has moved towards scenarios that will challenge EM assets. Thus far, there do not appear to be significant risk premia for US elections. We stay neutral EM local bonds overall and focus instead on idiosyncratic opportunities. They are OW India, Poland, Türkiye and Uruguay, offset by a short duration overlay and UWs in Chile and Thailand (EM Fixed Income Focus, Jul 18<sup>th</sup>).

## Credit

US HG flows rebound, led by ETFs. Total inflows into HG funds were \$31bn in June of which \$32bn were inflows to ETFs, a record while there was a modest \$1bn outflow from Mutual funds last month, the first monthly outflow since October 2023. Our estimates suggest that HG dealer inventories decreased from the last Fed reported data, especially at the <13m bucket which is near the lowest level in 3 months while the HY dealer inventories are the highest since early March 2023 (JPM Daily Credit Strategy, Jul 19<sup>th</sup>).

Introducing the J.P. Morgan Non-QM Prepayment Model, as investor focus has shifted to modeling prepayment and credit performance. Our non-QM prepayment model has a framework consistent with our agency and jumbo 2.0 models, which allows for relative value analysis. It accounts for additional characteristics specific to non-QM, such as doc type

and DSCR ratio. We reintroduce our framework to value the issuer call option in non-QM and walk through OAS across various deals, with and without step-ups (RMBS Credit Com mentary, Jul 12<sup>th</sup>).

Figure 1: Year-to-date returns %
![](images/dc9f7a0977a4a0c900dc298ce6f4a5b7efb9ecddc34ca4c70cd696d374ee5644.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan

EM corporate credit rating trend turned positive in 1H 2024 on net rating action volume, while the number of rating actions improved close to neutral. We have been expecting rating trends to turn positive in 2024 and continue to see more upside momentum for 2H24. Sovereign-driven rating actions contributed positively, while China real estate and Pemex remained drags. The rating trend improved for all regions during 1H 2024 except ME&A. Asia turned to a positive net rating volume for the first time since 2019 (EM Corporate Credit Ratings, Jul 18<sup>th</sup>).

## Currencies

Why not more USD strength? US elections featured prominently this week and betting market odds have shifted materially but USD is flat to softer on net in July. We attribute some of that to positioning, valuations and broadly-lower US yields. Our analysis complements the bullish-risk for USD around the elections via inflation and bear steepening risks. USD could benefit if trade policy were to successfully narrow the US’ current account deficit.

Trade Recommendations: Hold USD longs vs EUR, NZD. Unwind far strike of EUR/NOK put spread and hold long put given valuations. Stay long AUD/NZD on policy divergence. USD/CNH call spread expires out of the money. EM: We remain MW overall. Our bullish picks are TRY, ILS, ZAR, INR, PHP, SGD, MXN, UYU and bearish picks PLN, CZK, COP, CLP, CNY, THB, TWD (FXMW, Jul 19<sup>th</sup>).

## Commodities

Reassessing implications for Commodities under a Red Wave. There is an intent to reduce inflation by increasing domestic US oil production by 3 mbd via deregulation, bringing WTI oil prices to \$70/bbl and even \$60/bbl, making prices too low to incentivize production, potentially leading to a spike to \$100/bbl in the following year. US natural gas production could increase due to oil play and there could be even more certainty to swift end of Russia/Ukraine war thus increasing potential for curtailed Russian natural gas supply making it back to market. For agricultural markets, the return to tariffing may be emboldened, we look for stronger US growth, lower on-farm input costs and an eventual cease fire in Ukraine, which will take time to materialize into a normalization of agri export flows. China’s forward purchase commitments of US ag products are already at very low levels. We see value in long vol positions across the complex and on an index wide basis off the current lows, favoring sugar as an underlying (Global Commodities, Jul 17<sup>th</sup>).

Gold inflows continue to surge. Net inflows to gold markets were the highest in eight weeks. We remain bullish on gold prices into year-end, seeing prices rising towards \$2,500/oz in 4Q24 as the onset of a Fed cutting cycle restokes western investor demand via both futures and ETF holdings, driving the next leg higher in prices (Commodity Market Positioning & Flows, Jul 15<sup>th</sup>).

US power load growth likely to fuel natural gas demand growth by 2030. Data centers are expected to be the major driver for power demand growth in the US, accounting for \~55% of the forecasted 546 TWh demand increase and reaching \~10% of total US power demand by 2030. This translates into 2.5 Bcf/day increase in natural gas demand (out of an anticipated 4.6 Bcf/day increase in total US natural gas power demand growth). EVs, crypto-mining and all other things that need a socket to function are expected to drive another \~2.1 Bcf/day of natural gas power demand growth (Data center this, data center that, Jul 19<sup>th</sup>).

Global oil demand expands in July and global observable oil inventories continue to draw. US oil demand pulls back post-holidays as hurricane Beryl disrupts travel. YTD global oil demand continues to be on track compared with our November projections. The strength in demand we witnessed in high-frequency indicators since July 6 reflected in stronger jet fuel demand in China and Europe. Global observable oil inventories continue to record strong draws in July, more than three times faster than our implied balances show for the month (Oil Demand Tracker, Jul 17<sup>th</sup>).

## 2H24 disinflation: Partial and partial to the US

• Global disinflation to continue in 2H24 after 1H stall

• Fundamentals suggest core CPI to remain close to 3%

• Service price disinflation likely to be muted by firming goods prices

• A regional rotation is under way as US 2H24 slowing stands out

After decelerating sharply last year, the global disinflation process stalled in 1H24 (Table 1). A stall was incorporated in our forecast at the start of the year (Not so fast: Global core CPI to rise 3% in 1H24). In the event the 3.4%ar rise in global core CPI (ex. China and Türkiye) was higher than we had expected. We have been tracking global goods and service price dynamics separately and the anticipated end of a phase of core goods price disinflation was realized in 1H24. While core service price inflation was expected to moderate to a 4%ar, its acceleration to a 4.9%ar delivered the principal upside surprise in 1H24 performance.

As we look towards 2H24, the drivers of global goods and service prices are pointing in different directions. Global goods price pressures appear to be building, with a sharp rise in global transportation costs combining with a firming pricing power as global business spending picks up. At the same time, global service price inflation is moderating at midyear. We believe that this deceleration largely reflects the unwind of concentrated early year price increases. However, underlying fundamentals also point to disinflation ahead as service sector demand cools and wage inflation moderates.

With our forecast incorporating a 1%-pt deceleration in global service price inflation in 2H24, the net effect of this rotation will be to push global core CPI inflation lower in 2H24. Disinflation in service prices is, however, expected to remain incomplete as labor costs continue to rise at an elevated pace and the level of global service sector activity remains high relative to its pre-pandemic trend. As such, we expect global core inflation to remain close to a 3%ar in 2H24.

This global perspective masks an important relative performance shift taking hold. The US has experienced the strongest pandemic recovery among large economies, but its unemployment rate is up 0.5%-pt over the past year, a development likely attributable to strong labor supply gains. Thus, despite sustained strong employment gains, a downtrend in wage inflation is taking hold not evident elsewhere. This underlies our view that the US is poised to have the largest slide in 2H24 core inflation among major economies (Figure 1).

## Understanding the 1H24 disinflation stall

Following two years in which global core inflation hovered at a multi-decade high of close to 5%, a significant downshift took hold in 2023. Core inflation fell to a 3.2%ar in 2H23, significantly boosting expectations for central bank easing this year. In the event these expectations were scaled back as the disinflation process stalled in 1H24, with global core CPI (ex. China and Türkiye) rising at a 3.4%ar.

To an important degree the past year’s inflation dynamics have been driven by global impulses influencing goods price inflation (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Global core CPI, ex China and Türkiye
![](images/a8b6f670fc0527198ab2871ced2197fe033605ba55d40905f1c498e27057e0d1.jpg)
Source: National sources, J.P. Morgan. Details on request.

After running at a close to 5% rate in 2022, core goods prices stabilized in 2H23. A sharp and highly synchronized slide in goods price inflation was closely linked to the removal of pandemic-related supply chain dislocations and 2022 com-

%oya in specified CPI measure; triangles denote last 4qtrs modity price spikes. This point was reflected in last year’s drop in the FRBNY global supply chain pressure index. Last year’s weakness in global goods demand outside tech further reinforced this decline.

This disinflationary impulse faded as we turned in the new year. The supply chain pressure index stabilized, and renewed upward pressure on global transportation costs emerged. Global manufacturing activity also picked up. Our model forecasting global goods prices based on these metrics correctly anticipated an end of last year’s slide.

Given the sympathetic rise in core goods and service prices in the early stages of the pandemic recovery, it was natural that many forecasters anticipated that core service price inflation would follow last year’s goods inflation slide. However, the pass-through of swings in goods to service price inflation has historically been very modest during periods in which the two sectors face different macroeconomic impulses. And as we moved through 2023, the key impulses pointing to goods price disinflation were not present in service sector fundamentals. In particular,

Growth rotated towards services. Although the initial phase of the pandemic recovery was concentrated in the goods sector, a sharp sectoral rotation has taken place over the past two years with service activity moving above its pre-pandemic potential path while the manufacturing sector has become depressed (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Global real GDP, decomposed relative to potential Index, 4Q19=100; incl. 2Q24 est.
![](images/883855d5d659d27ad5982153b3d2a51af466dd48b1be2881df59017eb0b5f9c3.jpg)

Labor costs remain elevated. Experience shows labor market conditions are far more significant for service prices outcomes than goods (Figure 4). Because of a sustained strong service sector impulse, most indicators suggest that global labor markets remain tighter than their pre-pandemic position. Against this backdrop there has been no broad-based parallel to the normalization in manufacturing supply-chains for the service sector. Wage inflation has come off its highs but entered this year still running multiple percentage points higher than its prepandemic norm.

Figure 4: DM core CPI measures and wage growth
![](images/671dcd9e114275602729dd1d74c70a6a72001b4cc28c24321aae622431165e4a.jpg)
Source: National sources, J.P. Morgan. Details on request. Sample: 2001-2019.

There are also idiosyncratic national components driving service price inflation and they likely contributed to the 1H24 acceleration (Figure 5). The sharp upswing in Euro area core service price inflation can be attributed to an unwind of distortions and had been anticipated at the start of the year. More generally, we attribute the broader upside surprise in service price inflation in the US and EM ex. China to an early year bunching of price increases.

Figure 5: Core services CPI
![](images/78583cc6d84fa0f219168d33913cdeddec01bb82c9a6e2a85e77e8298a772469.jpg)

## A mid-year slide opens the door for easing

Although disinflation stalled in 1H24, recent months have seen a sharp unwind of an early year inflation spike. Indeed, June is tracking a second consecutive broad-based downside surprise, a deceleration that has pushed the three-month rate global core CPI below a 3%ar.

Short-term inflation swings have a high noise-to-signal ratio and having downplayed upward momentum earlier this year, it is appropriate to now caution against extrapolating this downshift. Having said that, by validating the transitory nature of the early year rise, recent reports shift the balance of risk for the path ahead. This validation is bolstering central bank confidence that inflation remains on a downward trajectory and is opening the door for a broad-based easing in global policy rates.

## Goods price pressure to build …

Looking through the recent up-and-down volatility in core inflation readings, 2H24 performance is likely to be determined by the interplay of divergent goods and service price impulses. Last year’s global core inflation slide largely reflected the normalization in global supply chains and unwind of commodity shocks. These same forces have now turned and point to a modest acceleration in 2H24 core goods price inflation. In particular,

Supply chain pressures reemerge. Transportation costs have moved materially higher in recent months across a wide range of shipping routes (Figure 6). While not nearly as sharp a rise as seen in 2022-23, the combination of this move with a more modest extension of delivery times seen in the global manufacturing PMI survey points to a firming in supply chain pressures in the coming months.

Global manufacturing on the mend. After a sluggish start to the year, the global industrial cycle looks to be turning up, boosted by resilient final demand and fading inventory drags. There are still reasons for caution, including a stall in the business and consumer confidence recovery, but we expect solid fundamentals to continue to support consumer spending. Readings for May and June show on net better news on final goods demand, and the message on production continues to firm.

Figure 6: Container freight rate and global output price PMIs Std dev from 2012-19 avg
![](images/eb27db51729d109232d3491ee52ae6fcf190fd973de6983dbbfc5a25b095a01e.jpg)

After the stabilization in 1H24, our model estimates point to a roughly 1%ar jump in global core goods inflation in 2H24 (Figure 7). Underlying this acceleration is firming in the global new orders PMI and the roughly 300% surge in global container shipping costs over the past six months, which are expected to pass through to consumer prices with a 3-6 month lag. Ongoing declines in US used car prices and China’s excess capacity are two disinflationary forces for consumer goods prices that are not directly captured by our model and could impart downside risk to our estimates in the near term. While US indicators point to further drops in auto prices, they also suggest that China’s deflationary impulse has faded (Figure 8).

Figure 7: Core goods CPI model, global ex Chn & Tur %3m, saar
![](images/1203b8a0a1d3eab153c5e7939faba0f0fd9c6b0be001e7e5a109546afb060eee.jpg)

Figure 8: US core goods and import prices
![](images/5143f4aa801731ead7b0ca2fede13add6da59eae46444b2bed14b83328781267.jpg)
Source: BLS, J.P. Morgan

## … while service price pressure eases

As noted earlier, we attribute the acceleration in 1H24 service price inflation to a front-loading of price increases early in the year against a fundamental backdrop of firm pricing power and tight labor markets. Our forecast for a significant deceleration in core service price inflation in 2H24 is linked to the view that the unwind of this front-loading should produce more modest 2H24 gains. However, we also see the macroeconomic environment becoming supportive of service price disinflation.

In this regard, our forecast incorporates a material slowing in global service sector activity gains (Figure 9). At a forecasted 2.1%ar service sector demand is anticipated to moderate by roughly a percentage point relative to its 2022-23 average pace. At the same time, DM wage inflation is anticipated to moderate to a 4% this year, a pace roughly a percentage point lower than its rate of increase this year.

Figure 9: Global GDP by sector %2q, saar
![](images/675aed52494ab05d96927d6c4b800749c7532d54e16e6ce44a0a343c9b00ba6a.jpg)

Figure 10: DM unemployment rate and wages
![](images/4dfa550b1e7fe61cb5af7ae5cded6742dd901734770c6b35d741ba23a41c9456.jpg)

## A relative easing in the US labor costs

An important shift in relative performance appears to be taking hold in the fundamental drivers of service price inflation. For much of the post-pandemic recovery the US has stood out relative to the rest of the world in delivering relatively strong demand and supply outcomes. Indeed, among major economies the US stands alone in delivering a recovery in which GDP has risen above its pre-pandemic potential path. At the same time, the US has seen its unemployment rate move up 0.5%-pt over the past year, a development likely attributable to strong labor supply gains.

A year ago, it was uncertain what impact these developments might have on labor costs. However, it now appears that US labor cost pressures are easing more rapidly than elsewhere. Despite sustained strong employment gains, a clearly established downtrend in US wage inflation is now in place (Figure 10, 11). Except in EM Asia, labor cost inflation remains close to cyclical highs across the globe (Figure 12)—most notably in the Euro area where wage gains continue to rise at a close to 5%ar.

Figure 11: DM wages
![](images/1836f4c1bb7000e7a76d9d0fa3e5721eb720c562edde33a16fc3ca9d7d1386f9.jpg)

Figure 12: Nominal wage growth
![](images/6d4842c9b4d63c45acc88399862c905dcef5d0a2ccf35f55d713e77f45d15310.jpg)

These factors have shifted the inflation risk profile in the direction of lower US relative outcomes. The trajectory of US wage inflation going forward remains unclear as the linkages between slack and labor bargaining power have shifted over time. With US employment costs yet to moderate along with the recent easing in labor market conditions (Figure 13), the upcoming ECI report (July 31) will send an important signal about the degree to which labor cost pressures are easing.

Figure 13: US quits rate and ECI wages, private sector
![](images/20c0c7047f951e2ec391be27135602b65d57e9a98c5a04272853973272e799ef.jpg)
Source: BLS, J.P. Morgan

## A midyear performance review of DM consumers

• DM consumer goods spending took a breather in 1H24 after rebounding in 2023

Healthy job growth, robust wage growth, fading inflation point to stronger consumer spending in 2H24

• Service spending is rising and the latest data show retail and motor vehicle sales lifting at midyear…

• ...but the fate of the expansion could lie in consumer sentiment, which looks to be damping outlays

Businesses will be a key driver of the cycle, in terms of capital investment but more importantly hiring (Figure 1). Amid already tight labor markets, job growth is keeping wage inflation high. For the expansion to continue, businesses need to tolerate some margin compression if inflation pressures are to abate. With already elevated margins holding up, businesses are still hiring at a solid clip. This keeps us constructive that global GDP can sustain trend-like growth through 2H24.

Figure 1: Business spending, Developed Markets
![](images/5a3b30da8eceb9c513c0c99772bf3d92b4a66964e0fc6429da608184450a5c1a.jpg)

Although businesses will play a central role, consumer spending—at roughly two-thirds of GDP—is where the rubber hits the road. We stress that for consumers: “Give them income and they will spend.” Spending tracks real income (Figure 2). However, the relationship has disconnected in the developed markets (DM). Goods spending rebounded in 2023 as real incomes recovered smartly in response to strong job growth and falling inflation. Robust real income gains on par with the strongest gains in the past two decades contrast, however, with a deceleration in DM goods spending in 1H24.

We remain constructive on the outlook for DM consumers, nevertheless. Retail sales look to be perking back up at midyear, while the post-pandemic recovery in services continues. The combination of healthy job growth, strong wage inflation, easing consumer price inflation, and robust balance sheets should provide sufficient purchasing power to sustain consumer spending well through the rest of this year. Con-

sumer goods spending also looks to be perking up in May and June, with the %3m reading bouncing back in May and a solid June reading from the US (Figure 3). Adding to this is an 11%ar jump in auto sales over the three months through June.

Figure 2: Real labor income and goods spending, Dev Mkt
![](images/21a9f4a53f223e67d9fecbc1b2165614d41ab1b63bc478670cc4d7614ceab2db.jpg)

We look for the overall DM consumer to expand at a trendlike 1.5%ar in 2H24. But a dark cloud hangs over this outlook: Consumer sentiment. Although sentiment has recovered from its 2022 lows, it remains well below its pre-pandemic highs and appears to be damping spending this year. With political uncertainty on the rise, the risk is that declining sentiment short-circuits the support from health fundamentals. In turn, this could force businesses to turn cautious, undermining the expansion.

Figure 3: Consumer spending, Developed Markets
![](images/8f45759388b6d8a40ddb8e73317d46f6d502ebc5afdbb6bada3157a87dfbba0b.jpg)

## A case of resilient consumers

Retail sales are the leading edge of the data for tracking the consumer and the overall health of aggregate demand. This owes to timely monthly reporting of the data across economies. In this regard, the deceleration in 1H24 consumer goods spending challenged our otherwise constructive view of a resilient expansion. After expanding 1.4% in 2023, DM retail sales look to have contracted 0.3%ar in 1H24 (Table 1). Much of the last year’s strength and this year’s slowdown owes to the US, but the picture of lackluster goods spending in recent quarters is broadly based (with the exception of the UK).

%q/q, saar (employment + wages (%oya) - inflation); Thru 2Q24

4Q19 = 100; financial wealth used for Jpn (thru 1Q21)

%ar (Sentiment std dev avg)
