The sluggish pace of goods spending masks broader health across all spending, however. Total real personal consumption expenditures are up 1.4%ar in 1H24. While this is still a step down from the average pace of the late post-GFC expansion, it is much more in line with the growth seen last year. Household spending on services appears still to be recovering from the rare slump seen in the pandemic (Figure 4).

Figure 4: Real consumer spending, Developed Markets Index, 2015-2019=100
![](images/9513e72f634185115cb48d262a0962965751a335444bc37a6c16115394a5b567.jpg)

It is not hard to be upbeat on the consumer. All the fundamentals point to strong spending growth in the DM. Employment growth has moderated from the boomy pace seen in the first three years of the expansion. But the pace of hiring remains solid (Table 1 and Figure 5). At the same time, wage inflation is running at nearly twice the pace seen over the last expansion. Nominal labor income in the DM is on track to surge nearly 6%ar in 1H24 (Figure 6). While inflation remains elevated it is well off its past highs. As such, real labor income is tracking a 2.9%ar gain in 1H24, a half-percentage stronger than in the later part of the last expansion.

Figure 5: Real labor income decomposition, Developed Markets %q/q, saar (wages %oya)
![](images/8165ac88dfc17080cbff1b26efa3cf87ec45ac6cd666d868efbd248cf3173b5d.jpg)

Figure 6: Labor income, Developed Markets
![](images/b0244086c6967d1accedeb4717959ab0f6ed3692bf99e3e70b21d8abed7cfd2d.jpg)

## Fortress balance sheets

Complementing strong labor income are healthy balance sheets. Excess saving has been worked off in the US, although remains extremely elevated across the other major developed economies. As of 1Q24, DM household wealth is up roughly 30% from its pre-pandemic level in 4Q19. While the US stands out, with wealth up nearly 40%, gains are broad-based across the DM (Figure 7). Wealth is up 21% in the Euro area and 30% in the UK. Japan has seen the smallest gain in household wealth since 4Q19 (17%), but the gain has accelerated with the recent ramp up in equity markets.

Figure 7: Wealth relative to pre-pandemic
![](images/425ea5c384aa97cc83501590b7ac13885d367b4e2a0517a5ecc1fcfbbc12bce1.jpg)

Figure 8: US real spending and wealth effects
![](images/db6a8cd88588b01354b339faa517e982cd1eecccc8ae15100db477022cf46f42.jpg)

The gains in wealth translate to consumer spending through the so-called “wealth-effect.” As households gain wealth, they can consume more out of their income. How much more? Traditionally, the wealth effect has averaged around \$0.035 for every \$1 gain in wealth in the US. This is consistent with a 3.5% permanent income “dividend” from the \$1 gain in wealth. Applying this rule-of-thumb to US wealth gains over the past two decades validates the wealth-effect’s usefulness as a guide to consumer spending (Figure 8). Since the start of the pandemic, US household wealth has soared \$44 trillion, \$12 trillion in just the last year through 1Q24. The wealth-effect suggest a boost to spending of 2.6%-pts annualized over the past year and continued—albeit fading— support through next year of 1%-2%-pts.

## Sour sentiment threatens outlook

The deceleration in consumer spending in 1H24 is at odds with the robust fundamentals. This is particularly so for consumer goods spending, which contracted outright in the DM in the first two quarters of the year. This disappointment can be traced to the depressed mood of consumers. It appears that households are unimpressed with multi-decade-high nominal income gains or even by the still-solid gains in real labor income discussed above. Not even the boomy stock market has been around to bolster sentiment.

Figure 9: Consumer sentiment and spending, Developed Market
![](images/eda9f0c90a40f96acd1281512657baf6e5cfc6775b27fb5207cea4cd583bf2d3.jpg)

The sour mood of consumers does not owe to the pandemic. After plunging in 2020, consumer sentiment recovered fully back to its pre-pandemic high by mid-2021 (Figure 9). The Russian invasion of Ukraine, supply chain snags, and surge in inflation pushed sentiment back to levels even lower than the worst of the pandemic. Although this recovered substantially, sentiment remains 0.8 standard deviation below its 2010-19 average. Consumer goods spending has tracked sentiment remarkably well through this period. The low level of sentiment appears to be the main culprit for the slide back in spending growth over the past six months.

Figure 10: Consumer sentiment St dev from 2010-2019 avg; Both scales
![](images/1300ee8dbaf6a4dc1a814152aa12b891e63f8d70cce0cb5e2e739e724e31e49a.jpg)

Sentiment is depressed broadly (Figure 10). A strong argument can be made for European households to be worried about the conflicts in Ukraine and Israel. But the recovery in sentiment has been strongest in Europe. As of 1H24, sentiment is roughly the same across the major DM economies. More surprising is that, once accounting for the state of the macroeconomic fundamentals, sentiment remains deeply depressed.

Figure 11: Consumer animal spirits
Deviation of sentiment from modeled output (income, inflation, u-rate, stk mkt)
![](images/0210810470dd64f583da71e2d61ebf6a2e990a423758b8961e7c9a0061eb25e2.jpg)

In recent work, we developed tracker for “animal spirits.” Specifically, animal spirits are defined as the deviation in consumer sentiment from what can be explained by nominal income growth, the level and change of the unemployment rate, inflation, and stock market gains. Updating this model, animal spirits are not only low but have not been recovering of late (Figure 11). Given the role of sentiment in spending, the depressed state of animal spirits underscores the risk to consumer spending in 2H24.

## Shipping cost surge points to firming goods CPI

After a full reversal from the pandemic-era surges, shipping costs have moved materially higher again

While supply chain pressures remain modest, the shipping cost surge is being reinforced by signs of firming pricing amid improving demand

Our models point to a 1%ar rise in core goods inflation in 2H24, up from 0%ar in 1H24, but several factors could damp the rebound

Last year’s global core inflation slide was closely linked to a normalization in global supply chains and unwind of the 2022 commodity price shocks. With support from a weak global manufacturing sector, global goods prices stabilized late last year and averaged 0%ar through 1H24. While we have been expecting stable goods price inflation, cost pressures are now building and global dynamics related to goods price inflation are shifting upward amid improving trends in manufacturing activity and a renewed surge in global shipping costs.

Global transportation costs had seen a near-complete reversal from pandemic-driven surges, but have moved materially higher again in recent months across a wide range of shipping routes. While not nearly as sharp a rise as seen in 2021-22,the cumulative increase over the past six months is substantial and futures point to prices staying higher for longer. The combination of this move with a more modest extension of delivery times seen in the global manufacturing PMI survey points to a firming in supply chain pressures in the coming months (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Container freight rate and global supply chain pressures
![](images/cecf468c30c81a630e28246e53c26c2e8e58e0e3d684db918ce1d807d5005c9e.jpg)

A part of the recent rise in shipping costs reflects ongoing geopolitical-driven supply shocks related to tensions in the Middle East impacting the Red Sea. However, improving goods demand is now likely also adding to the upward pressure. In contrast to the start of the year––when goods demand was still sluggish––the latest spike in global shipping costs is accompanied by improving trends in manufacturing demand as early readings for May-June show mixed but on net better news on final goods demand, and the message on production continues to firm. This firming is evident in a broader set of goods price indicators, including the output price PMI (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Global manufacturing PMI, delivery times and prices DI, sa; both scales
![](images/9fe5390c9e694ce8271f531e22836790ed2d78b9b987098487971c56149051ae.jpg)

## Shipping costs and delivery times rising...

The rise in shipping costs at the turn of the year was sparked by geopolitical tensions in the Middle East and drought in Central America that materially disrupted global maritime trade through the Suez and Panama Canals. While drought-related concerns have eased and transits through the Panama Canal have started to normalize, geopolitical risks remain elevated as the Red Sea crisis remains unresolved. Continued pressure from the Houthi rebels has forced shipping companies to continue to re-route around Africa with traffic through the Suez Canal 65%-75% lower than mid-December levels (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Cargo transit trade volumes
![](images/831164bba4b0900383070e39d8662b2d450f52d969ac100f407d44f66d3251c8.jpg)

Upward pressure in recent months is evident across a wide range of widely used container shipping cost indexes, including the Drewry World Container Index and the Shanghai Containerized Freight Index. The more volatile Baltic Dry Index (BDI)––a shipping cost measure for bulk goods including foodstuff, metals, ores, coal, etc.—has also trended higher (Figure 4). But as we have noted in our earlier work, the BDI tends to have more of an immediate impact on raw material and intermediate goods prices than manufactured goods pric-

Figure 4: Global container shipping rates
![](images/a1ac6f76a6395767b9f2a829762b8850f7fd9c762e439b50331c35052ad7687b.jpg)

The aggregate measures of container shipping costs are now four times their early-December levels. Prices along routes that would typically go through the Suez Canal—particularly from Asia to Europe—have surged even more, by sixfold. Containerized shipping costs from Asia to the US are up about threefold, with slightly larger gains for routes to the east coast than to the west coast. Costs from Europe to Asia have risen much less, by about 35% (Figure 5).

Figure 5: Increase in shipping costs by route
![](images/9a6ef4cd051fd9b6a3d137c3906d3565a5f6e60a4a688088464c6a986bd11b0a.jpg)

In January we anticipated that the excess supply of container ships globally—many of which were ordered during the pandemic period and were expected to enter service this year–– would likely help to mitigate the fallout from the Red Sea crisis to global supply chains. However, with the re-routing of vessels around Africa’s Cape of Good Hope lasting for around six months, capacity shortages have more than offset scheduled new vessel deliveries for 2024 by now. The ensuing supply bottlenecks have led to increased port congestion in key hubs such as Singapore and longer average vessel waiting times.

After the unwinding of global supply chain dislocations had reversed a sizeable lengthening of delivery times (and output prices) through early last year, these measures have all firmed modestly in recent months, likely amplified by the recent disruptions in global trading routes. The latest PMI readings underscore this message. The global delivery times PMI dipped last month, suggesting an extension of time-to-market, reinforcing the turn back up in the output price PMI and, in turn, a fading in much of the deflationary momentum in goods prices globally.

Figure 6: Manufacturing PMI suppliers' delivery times DI, sa (down means longer delivery times)
![](images/39bbbc789853a3a1fa43a4008a5995b00c2b819da925c34ee1a7fdc328ba1ee7.jpg)

Since about mid-2023 the delivery times indexes for the US and Euro area have started to lengthen again, although current levels remain consistent with their long-run averages suggesting no meaningful fallout to global supply chains (Figure 6). At the same time, the global manufacturing PMIs show a renewed rise in backlogs and renewed widening in the gap between purchases and finished goods inventories (Figure 7). Part of this could reflect orders being rolled forward in anticipation of major trade policy changes––including large-scale tariff increases––after the November US elections, which are raising demand from shippers on top of reduced capacity. A similar dynamic of orders being rolled forward, albeit on a much larger scale, was observed during the pandemic-induced supply-chain disruption.

Figure 7: Global manufacturing PMIs, supply bottleneck indicators DI, sa (low means longer delivery times) DI-poin
![](images/29f74a1a048db582c2dc0c49792d64af159963e0062c07117a4d8b89a2a241a3.jpg)

## ...raising pressure on goods prices

Increases in shipping costs have started to pass through to imported goods prices with a lag. However, the impact on consumer prices is not clear-cut and will depend not only on the duration and intensity of the disruptions but also the strength of demand. Cyclical factors can either reinforce or mitigate the upward pressure on prices. During a period of weaker consumer demand, a greater portion of upward pressure on shipping costs could be absorbed by profit margins than reflected in higher final output prices.

The impact of higher shipping costs on consumer goods prices has been modest so far as core goods inflation averaged 0%ar in 1H24––a rate that is below its pre-pandemic norm. This is likely in part because the early-year rise in shipping costs was viewed as transitory while China’s excess capacity and still sluggish final goods demand continued to damp goods prices. During this period, the FRBNY global supply chain pressure index remained soft, close to its historical norms.

As we look to 2H24, the impulses shaping core goods inflation that had pointed to a steadying price profile for 2024 are becoming more mixed, and a modest firming in goods prices appears likely. As noted, the PMI survey of output prices has firmed alongside better manufacturing activity and the surge in shipping costs above their early-year peaks. Meanwhile, China’s deflationary impulse has faded judging by the levelling off of trading partner import prices following their earlier steep declines (Figure 8).

Figure 8: US and Euro area import prices from China
![](images/4067467a0fd855114a89528f227e64aec2b979e422a446075c97836e810df064.jpg)

## Models point to 2H24 goods CPI lift

Over the past few years we have looked at the New York Fed’s global supply chain pressure index (GSCPI) as a simple tool to measure the inflationary impact of supply chain dislocation. While the GSCPI includes a range of shipping prices (BDI, Harpex and US airfreight costs), it does not incorporate information from the shipping cost measures that have been most sensitive to the recent disruptions. It also seeks to isolate supply disturbances from demand effects by conditioning prices on demand variables from the PMI surveys.

Our model, which estimates the lagged impacts of the FRBNY supply chain pressure index, the global new orders PMI and container shipping costs on global core goods prices, points to a pickup in global core goods inflation in 2H24 to around 1%ar––a move that would add around 0.3%-pt to global core inflation (Table 1). While the model has done a good job of capturing the ups and downs in goods price inflation since the pandemic, it has worked less well in tracking swings prior to 2019. Additionally, while it correctly signalled the slide in goods inflation last year, the fall-off came roughly six months later than the model suggested (Figure 9).

Figure 9: Core goods CPI model, global ex Chn & Tur %3m, saar
![](images/46f3cb18039231bc43cf21cdbb6ab52f3913f7985fcc95f2aacd2d354c052c09.jpg)

Table 1: Consumer goods CPI model (global ex Chn & Tur) %3m saar, Sample 2012-2023. t-stats in parentheses
<table><tr><td></td><td>Model 1</td><td>Model 2</td></tr><tr><td>Supply Chain Pressure Index (-4)</td><td>0.64 (5.3)</td><td>0.75 (6.6)</td></tr><tr><td>New orders PMI (-1)</td><td>0.15 (5.2)</td><td>0.17 (5.6)</td></tr><tr><td>World Container Index (-4)</td><td>0.21 (7.0)</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Shanghai Container Index (-4)</td><td></td><td>0.55</td></tr><tr><td>R-sq</td><td>0.74</td><td>(6.6) 0.74</td></tr><tr><td>Std err</td><td>1.15</td><td>1.15</td></tr></table>

Source: J.P. Morgan Global Economics. FRBNY, Drewry, S&P Global. Details on request. Model also includes constant (not shown). Supply chain pressure index and Container shipping indexes are standard deviations from their historical averages. The new order PMI is a diffusion index.

The steep declines in US used car prices and China’s excess capacity are two disinflationary forces for consumer goods prices that are not directly captured by our model. While the former is largely idiosyncratic, its has a material influence on the global aggregate: global core goods CPI excluding US autos was up 0.9%ar in the three months to June but was flat including US autos (Figure 10). China’s export price spillover is an important global impulse for manufactured goods prices that matters for a broad set of countries. While US indicators point to further drops in auto prices, China’s deflationary impulse has faded. Intensification of either of these disinflationary trends could pose downside risk to the model estimates in the near term.

Figure 10: Core goods CPI %3m, saar
![](images/4d4963545d43d3417c567d75704ad004da9ee747f4d55272053c31d7dd0430c9.jpg)

## US: Oh no Mr. Bill—the first Sahm rule was triggered

• The unemployment dynamics described by the Sahm rule were previously discussed by Bill Dudley

While the Dudley rule is only slightly different from the Sahm rule, it has been triggered “on” recently

• There are good reasons why this time may be different, but that’s no cause for complacency

A casual glance at the series for the US unemployment rate tells you that it does not display symmetric wiggles around a trend—rather it is characterized by long periods of slow declines punctuated by short, sharp upward movements (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Unemployment rate
![](images/5ae7f84880f449ce6b7049c74256b65f24e64835630dc68a34abeb013b47c668.jpg)
Source: BLS, J.P. Morgan

This asymmetry has been well documented for at least 40 years.[1] Recently it has been popularized in a rule of thumb developed by Claudia Sahm, now called the Sahm rule, which holds that every time the three-month moving average of the unemployment rate is 0.50%-pt or more above the lows of that series over the prior 12 months, then the economy is in, or is about to enter, a recession.

In June, the 3-month average unemployment rate was 0.46%-pt above the low of that series over the prior year—in other words, very close to, but not triggering the Sahm rule threshold (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Unemployment rate relative to recent low 3mma, 12-m trailing low
![](images/a182aa8b569da1d2e89a699f0962e6ffc0fd6af9f5e3f88e32d7cf82d15d9846.jpg)

Well before the Sahm rule became popular, former NY Fed president Bill Dudley would often cite another regularity that was very similar to the Sahm rule: when the 3-month moving average of the unemployment rate was at least 0.3%-pt above the low of that series over any time during the expansion, then the economy was in a recession or would enter a recession within six months.

So while the Sahm rule had a lookback over the past year, the Dudley rule looked back over the cycle. At 4.0%, the current three-month average unemployment rate is 0.5%-pt above the cycle low of 3.5%, which according to the Dudley rule means the economy will soon be in recession.

## True in practice but not in theory

How worried should we be about the Dudley rule being triggered “on”? One observation Dudley also used to make is that standard economic theory doesn’t give any reason why the unemployment rate can’t go up a little bit, say, 0.5-1.0%-pt, and not go up further. (Of course, if you give clever economists a fact, they can back out a model to explain, as is done here.)

Returning to the Sahm rule, do we see violations of that rule in other countries? If we limit ourselves to Anglosphere economies (due to similarities of labor market institutions) we do find occasional instances when the unemployment rate goes up in the 0.5%-1.0% range, but no more—for example in Canada at the time of the US dot-com bubble (Figure 3).

Figure 3: Canadian unemployment rate %, bars indicate when Sahm rule is triggered
![](images/ad504ec2d9efbbfb138e6d6e10f83f8063db9dcb147eac504d1c3d680157f470.jpg)
Source: Statistics Canada, J.P. Morgan

Australia had a similar experience at that time, and one can make the case that the UK dodged the Sahm rule during the pandemic (though by all accounts that was an unusual cycle for all economies). So looking at comparable international experiences, there are exceptions to the Sahm rule, but they are rare. The similarities may be due to common shocks, though that seems more applicable to Canada than to Australia or the UK.

Another this-time-is-different argument points to labor hoarding behavior, leading firms to be reluctant to lay off workers, which were hard to hire over the last four years. The data confirm that layoffs have remained near cycle lows in recent months (Figure 4).

Figure 4: The JOLTS series Thousands, sa, both scales
![](images/8ac7ee93095b9434cdf1e54046a7120b3f97d55e2ea21b5f0f5aa65ccc59e6b3.jpg)
Figure 6: Claims and hiring Thousands, sa, both scales

However, this alone may not be enough to prevent a rise in unemployment. Almost two decades ago University of Chicago economist Robert Shimer found that “the job finding probability has accounted for three-quarters of the fluctuations in the unemployment rate in the United States and the employment exit probability for one-quarter” and that “The job finding rate is strongly procyclical. The employment exit probability is weakly countercyclical.”

In a note from around the same time we found something similar in relation to jobless claims. Whereas claims are often seen as a proxy for layoffs we found that instead they correlate better with hiring (itself a correlate of Shimer’s job finding probability). The reason being that claimants didn’t just lose their job but also haven’t found another job.

Updating that analysis confirms the earlier results. From the start of the JOLTS series in 2000 until just before the pandemic, layoffs explain 44% of the variation in jobless claims (Figure 5).

Figure 5: Claims and firing Thousands, sa, both scales
![](images/fd90cc6c71cae17607d1ea9fb79c862b72019ea8f469a897595ffa39670ce8ca.jpg)

Over the same period the gross hires measure explains 72% of the variation in claims (Figure 6).

![](images/9a32bd3410c517cec9e536acbc44a72e9093587283ab8c796bc3af4142827420.jpg)

Both Shimer’s work and our work on claims suggest that even if layoffs remain muted, unemployment can still drift higher if gross hiring (and the associated job finding probability) continues to drift lower.

Several economic series have displayed unique and unprecedented behavior in this business cycle (e.g., the speed of disinflation in the absence of a recession). So it isn’t merely wishful thinking to think that triggering the Dudley rule won’t necessarily be followed by a further rise in unemployment. The historical record here and abroad argues against complacency, however, particularly as long as hiring rates are trending lower.

[1]“Are economic time series asymmetric over the business cycle?,” Salih N. Neftci, Journal of Political Economy, Vol 2, No 2 (Apr, 1984)

## Connecting Brazil’s fiscalmonetary dots

Fiscal policy has played an important role in Brazil, affecting inflation, expectations, and risk premium

We assess its impact by estimating counterfactual CPI, GDP, and SELIC without the fiscal shocks

Our model suggests CPI approaching 2.3%oya in 2Q;
GDP growth would be 0.5%-pt lower than last year...

• … and the SELIC rate would be 1%-3%-pts lower than the current 10.50%

Over the long-run, fiscal shocks tend to bring lower GDP, leaving higher policy rate and price level

Fiscal policy plays an important role in a variety of ways such as to help stimulate growth during recessions, reduce social and economic inequalities, and correct market imbalances. But sometimes fiscal policy can overheat an economy, and push up inflation that requires higher policy rates. We study this latter aspect through a Structural VAR that shows the contribution of the recent fiscal policy on Brazil’s inflation, GDP growth and interest rates.

We find that the deterioration of the primary balance from a surplus of 2% of GDP in mid-2022 to below a 2% deficit until 2Q24 had meaningful impact on Brazil’s economy. Absent this shock, instead of higher and persistent, inflation would be below BCB’s target at the cost of 0.5%-pt lower GDP growth (2.5% instead of 3% last year). Reflecting the weaker growth and inflation, the SELIC rate would be 1%-3%-pts lower (Figure 1). Looking further into the future, rising fiscal deficits do not have a positive impact on GDP growth but result in higher price levels and interest rates.

Figure 1: Economic variables without fiscal shock\*
%-change relative to current data (1Q for GDP and 2Q for inflation and SELIC)
![](images/c2f355995b0df620044f2c5552d8e8bf9783f5ba518a9612ad4484fa282f7aae.jpg)

## The fiscal shock and its consequences

Brazil’s primary balance (ex. interest payments) has undergone large shifts since the pandemic. In 2020, the economic recession and the need for fiscal support, particularly to

households, widened the deficit almost to double digits. With the economy and commodity prices recovering alongside some curbing of expenditure growth, the primary balance turned into the first surplus in almost a decade. But the tax cuts implemented in 2022, subsequent rise of real expenditures, and one-off court-debt payment (known as precatórios) pushed it back deep into negative territory (Figure 2).

![](images/abd3abc407b53bf0dc71185deec9d4261d39401c848ed8c84c0ed21032e02ef6.jpg)

The sharp ups and downs in fiscal policy have affected the economy significantly. But it is hard to disentangle the twoway feedback between the primary balance versus GDP growth and inflation. To separate the interconnections, we restrict the contemporaneous cross-effect between economic and fiscal variables to zero (see Appendix). In lieu of a negative restriction often seen in the literature to identify fiscal shocks, we assume a more parsimonious strategy.

The historical decomposition of the data shows that the sharp shift in the primary result led inflation to be higher than it would otherwise have been. In our calculations, headline CPI would be running between 2% and 2.5% last quarter, instead of rising close to 4% (Figure 3). This means that inflation could have been below the target at this point of the economic cycle.

Figure 3: Headline CPI and counterfactual without fiscal shocks
![](images/05817fe031640bf16ae5cac827e195d9690694459efffb2389b4b6412ba66493.jpg)

The flip side is that while inflation could have been better behaved, GDP growth would have been lower. The central bank’s estimate of the output gap is currently around balance for 2Q24 but our model shows that without the fiscal expansion the output gap would be negative at -0.4%. Translating this into current GDP growth, the counterfactual for last year’s 2.9% GDP growth would be 2.4% (Figure 4).

Figure 4: Output gap and counterfactual without fiscal shocks %
![](images/b32cd687e81fb803389dc3ca106caa9856e7431a9c590153b50ada99ed1dfc98.jpg)

## The fiscal-monetary connection

We look at how this counterfactual economic backdrop would impact interest rates in two ways. First, we can assume that the BCB follows a Taylor rule to set the policy rate. Our estimates show that interest rates would have been 1%-pt lower in a standard specification of the rule and 1.3%-pts in J.P. Morgan’s country-specific Taylor rule. The SELIC rate would then already be at one digit considering the lower output gap and inflation, all else constant.

But all else is not constant. In fact, the fiscal stimulus amid concerning debt-to-GDP dynamics also contributes to a more depreciated exchange rate and higher inflation expectations, which in turn signal another leg up on inflation ahead. So our second way of looking at this is through our in-model estimate of the SELIC rate in a world without the fiscal shocks, which shows the policy rate below the estimated nominal neutral around 8% (Figure 5).

Figure 5: SELIC and counterfactual without fiscal shocks
![](images/a79c6b289cef3aad44f54b636608de369be5c580fbca119183de20195dc23873.jpg)

This exercise quantitatively shows that the recent fiscal expansion had positive impact on economic growth. We have found in our research that the private sector balance sheets are healthy, and the fiscal stimulus is, in our view, partly responsible for that.

And yet, there is a cost to this extra growth push. In Brazil’s most recent case, this cost is stubborn inflation that could have been below the target. But because fiscal stimulus works against the disinflation process, the inflation-targeter central bank must keep rates higher than they otherwise would have been. As a result, the fiscal stimulus becomes negative for GDP growth over the long term, cumulatively leaving a higher price level despite the higher policy rate (Figure 6).

Figure 6: Fiscal shock impact in CPI and output gap %-points impact of a 1%-point primary shock
![](images/464c0b0d98a09731b73bbd458c5d734b30653b07ea65cd8d9a2201d033750233.jpg)

## Appendix: details of the model

Our model has four variables. The fiscal shock is represented by the seasonally-adjusted quarterly primary result as a % of GDP. To accommodate the changes to Brazil’s inflation target, we specify the CPI as deviations of the target. The monetary policy variable is the SELIC rate minus 1-year ahead inflation expectations and the neutral rate estimated by BCB. Finally, the economic growth variable is the output gap also estimated by BCB and available in the 2Q Inflation Report. Once the counterfactuals are estimated, we back up GDP growth, output gap, headline CPI, and the SELIC rate without the fiscal shocks.

The model is estimated with quarterly data over the period 2005Q1–2024Q2. We impose restrictions of zero only in the relationship between the contemporaneous primary shock, GDP, and inflation. This restriction applies only to contemporaneous effects and there are no others imposed, including for the monetary policy shocks and reaction to the other variables.

The signals of the shocks are in line with economic theory. In the short run, fiscal consolidation leads to lower CPI, lower GDP and SELIC rate. Inflation or GDP shocks raise the policy rate and the fiscal result, but while higher GDP means higher CPI, higher CPI leads to lower GDP. Finally, a higher SELIC reduces the primary result, inflation and GDP growth.

## Australia: Job ad-justments

• Measures of job openings have declined different extents over the past two years

• The variation seems to reflect differences in the scope of the measures

• Online job ads appear to under-represent low skilled jobs, which have been strong this cycle

• Adjusting for differences implies labour demand continues to cool, but remains stronger than pre-2020

While the major measures of Australian job openings have fallen from their cycle highs (Figure 1), there are significant differences in the degree of retracement. Two popular highfrequency measures of online job ads, SEEK and ANZ-Indeed, are close to pre-pandemic levels and so have been taken as foreshadowing more significant weakening in the labour market than implied by the ABS’s job vacancy survey. In this piece, we investigate the drivers of the divergences across job openings indices. We find some evidence that measures of online job ads overstate the decline in openings, as they tend to under-represent low skilled jobs.

Figure 1: Evolution of Australian job opening measures Index, 1Q20 = 100
![](images/218606d11268cb6f5b461f5e3a9e73c74b6bab6849a92037ff1811b87d17f9dd.jpg)

## Depends who you ask

The main reasons for the variation across measures of job openings are differences in scope and composition. Benchmark measures of openings are typically based on a representative sample of firms. The ABS job vacancies series records the number of vacancies on the third Friday of the middle month of the quarter from a sample of about 4000 firms. The survey is designed to be representative of the structure of the broader labor market, and is a measure of the stock of vacancies outstanding on a given day.

The ANZ-Indeed survey is also a stock measure of openings; the series takes an average of the number of job ads across different sites on a given day of the month. In contrast, both the internet vacancy index (IVI) and SEEK’s job ad index are flow measures of openings, and record the number of new job ads per month. In principle, we shouldn’t expect the flow

indices to accumulate to the stock measures. Differences will vary over time too, if the average duration of a vacancy varies across the cycle.

But, even focusing only on the two flow measures, there’s currently a good deal of dispersion between the SEEK index and the IVI. This is in spite of the fact that job ads on SEEK are included in the IVI (the IVI data counts newly lodged job ads on SEEK, CareerOne and Workforce Australia). Unlike the ABS survey, online job ads aren’t necessarily representative of the broader labour market, and differences in the distribution of ads posted across dimensions like regions, industry and skill level can produce variation across series.

Figure 2: SEEK job ads vs. internet vacancy index across states % of Jan 2020 level
![](images/681a2ba26b3f2b4e55cb7a5b1305356d6bd523435d99c49888fc81dc1d004b05.jpg)

We first look to see if aggregate differences in the IVI and SEEK indices owe to differences in performance across Australian states. Figure 2 plots the state-level indices for both the SEEK and IVI measures as a percentage of their January 2020 levels. In every state, the IVI measure of job ads is above the SEEK measure as a percent of its pre-pandemic value. The size of the divergence between the two measures in each state is also very similar to the aggregate divergence, implying that differences in geographical coverage of the measures are not responsible for the current divergence.

![](images/81669865681173b9470baa85a08cec0fa6d94e429c9c7592efba6e33a8be4ba2.jpg)

Differences between SEEK and IVI might also reflect differences in trend growth between the series. If trend growth in the IVI is higher than SEEK, this would help to explain the difference between the two series when benchmarked to their

2020 levels. Figure 3 shows the two series after accounting for the 2006-19 trends. After de-trending, the gap between the two series actually widens because we only find evidence of positive trend growth over 2006-19 in the SEEK series.

Finally, current differences might reflect the measures being skewed to different skills/industries. The IVI provides a breakdown of online job ads by skill level (where 1 is the highest skilled and 5 the lowest), which we use to try and determine which skill levels the SEEK data tend to over/ underweight relative to the IVI data. Specifically, we use a LASSO regression to choose the weighted combination of the IVI skill level series that best forecasts the aggregate SEEK index on a pre-pandemic sample (Jan 06 - Dec 19).

Figure 4: Coefficients on skill levels in SEEK job ad index Coefficient on skill leve
![](images/66b7178341d1620c529f01edd5f75a1ca53d64dfb7524419a1bec577f67d3112.jpg)

Figure 4 plots the coefficients on skill levels from the LASSO regression. The large negative coefficient on skill level 5 implies the SEEK index reacts inversely to increases in openings of the lowest skill level. This suggests the SEEK index probably underweights low skill jobs relative to the IVI (the actual proportion of skill level 5 openings is roughly equal to proportion of both level 3 and 4 skills in the IV index over the same period). Figure 5 shows that skill level 5 ads have outperformed since 2020, so it’s plausible the under-representation of low skill jobs in the SEEK index is creating some downward bias in the series at the moment.

Figure 5: Lowest skill job ads vs. other skill levels over pandemic Index, Jan 2020 = 100
![](images/344c4fbae48587a360e30f90648b62dac47cbd45fd29a3a76d70a3123f7a1309.jpg)

Based on these results, we should fade some of the negative signal coming from the SEEK index currently. More broadly, the exercise suggests we should not place too much weight on individual measures of online job ads, as they may be subject to representative biases, if they tend to over/underweight certain sectors of the economy. The ABS survey is subject to sampling biases (e.g. non-response) and has been subject to data collection issues in the past; the survey was suspended over the crucial window of 2008-09, but should do a better job at representing labour demand across the economy.

## Necessary adjustments

A downside of the ABS survey is that it is only available quarterly. ANZ-Indeed’s job ad series is monthly but, like other measures of online job ads, may suffer from representative biases: Online job ads are costly to post, thus can be biased towards higher skilled openings. The ANZ-Indeed measure has also changed sources over time. In 2018, data from Indeed were first incorporated while newspaper data were suspended. In 2022, data from SEEK were dropped.

Figure 6: Ratio of ANZ-Indeed job openings to ABS vacancies Ratio
![](images/bff42a57ba78f5cc42f13f375d5806fc7be84031fd21676d74c2b1535a2a27b0.jpg)

These discrepancies mean that the ANZ-Indeed series is unlikely to match the ABS vacancy measure in level terms. However, if the biases are constant over time, we can difference them away. One way to check this is to plot the ratio of the ANZ-Indeed series to the ABS: a constant ratio would indicate the bias is time-invariant. Figure 6 shows the ratio has varied through time (we start the sample after the ABS survey was restarted in 2010), indicating differences between the series have varied through time. But it appears that a regime shift occurred between 2018-2020, the same time the ANZ measure was altered to include Indeed data, and the ratio has been fairly stable outside those periods.

Based on the relationship between the series since 2020, scaling up the ANZ-Indeed measure by about a quarter should roughly align the level of the series with the ABS survey and provide a higher frequency update on economy wide vacancies. After making this adjustment, the latest data imply that vacancies declined further in June, but labor demand remains stronger than prior to the pandemic.

## United States

• All signs still point to a first Fed ease at the September FOMC meeting

• Retail sales picked up in June, easing earlier concerns about consumer spending

• A slow rise in the number on unemployment benefits points to a cooling job market

• Next week’s 2Q GDP report expected to show trendlike 2.0% growth

The soft landing story has been gaining increasing credibility in recent weeks. If anything, some of the growth data had been suggesting activity was slowing more than would be desirable. This had spurred speculation that the Fed could cut rates as soon as the late July meeting. Two things happened this week to check that chatter. First, Chair Powell spoke on Monday and gave no hint that the FOMC is in any rush to cut rates. While obviously pleased by the recent inflation data, the Fed chief indicated that the Committee was taking a considered approach to the first cut, which is more consistent with a September ease. Second, the economic data this week generally came in better than expected, suggesting activity retained good momentum heading into 2Q.

Among that activity data the most notable upside surprise was the June retail sales report. Before this report, 2Q consumer spending looked to be growing at an annual rate similar to the 1.5% outturn registered in 1Q. Instead, June retail sales increased a peppy 0.8% (taking out the volatile auto and gas components) and estimates for the prior two months were revised higher. With these data in hand, real consumption growth last quarter now looks to have expanded at around a 2.0% pace, which is pretty close to a Goldilocks outcome.

The latest housing and manufacturing data also offered some better news. The only cautionary signal in this week’s data came from jobless claims. Not only did initial claims for unemployment insurance jump in the latest week, but ongoing claims for jobless benefits continued climbing to the highest since late ’21. As we discuss in this week’s research note, a slowing in gross hiring can push up claims even when layoff activity is limited (as is likely the case now).

Well, we’ve managed to make it all the way to the fourth paragraph of this essay before talking about the presidential election. But that topic continues to dominate discussions about the medium-term economic outlook. In a wide-ranging interview about economic policy published this week, Republican nominee Trump was asked if he would let Fed chair Powell serve the remainder of his term, to which he answered “I would let him serve it out, especially if I thought he was

doing the right thing.” When asked about Fed rate cuts before the election, Trump stated “It’s something they know they shouldn’t be doing.” In a webinar this week with Sarah Binder, a noted scholar of Fed politics, we discussed various ways the White House can exert influence on the central bank.

The Fed will be quiet next week ahead of the FOMC meeting the following week. But the data flow will not be quiet. Much of the remaining June data will be released, including consumer spending, durable goods orders, and new and existing home sales. Thursday brings the first look at 2Q real GDP; we expect a modest acceleration from the 1.5% growth pace in 1Q to 2.0% last quarter. Friday’s June core PCE inflation report is expected to show a 0.2%m/m increase in prices last month, leaving the year-ago increase unchanged at 2.6%.

## Consumers get their mojo back

Retail sales were stronger than expected in June, and we see 2Q real consumer spending tracking 2.0%q/q saar, a bit stronger than our prior assumption before the report. Total nominal retail sales and food services spending was unchanged in the month, dragged down by declines for sales of motor vehicles and at gas stations, but excluding those categories spending rose a solid 0.8%m/m. The control category (ex auto, gasoline, building materials, and food services) also increased 0.9%m/m compared to expectations for a 0.2-0.3% gain, and there were upward revisions to May and April (Figure 1).

![](images/33a9996a6156421c9569e5dada65d20884991fabbfa8836ce45c4ce34fa82360.jpg)

Based on this report we look for real consumer spending to have risen 0.2%m/m last month. The 2.0%q/q pace for the whole quarter is slower than the 3%-plus pace in 2H23, but an acceleration from the 1.5% growth in 1Q. The latest number should thus ease concerns about an abrupt loss of momentum in consumer spending .

Looking across some of the other categories of spending, food services rose 0.3%m/m and the prior month’s 0.4% drop was revised to a 0.4% increase. Spending at restaurants had been quite soft from December through March but has risen in each of the last three months. Sales at building material

stores, which have been hampered by the slowdown in the housing market, increased a decent 1.4%m/m. Sales were also up for most categories of spending within the control group.

## Homebuilding still trending down

Housing starts and permits rebounded in June, led by the vol atile multifamily segment, while single-family activity continued to moderate. Single-family construction is likely pull ing back temporarily in response to the recent rise in inventories, while multifamily construction was coming off a low base. Builders had already sharply cut starts in prior months as they contend with an elevated pace of new deliveries (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Multifamily units under construction and completions
![](images/7fe5b9125be5d23b756719278c03dffd9d580c662b4b9bfeb01361e29dcf1847.jpg)

Both starts and permits are down substantially in the second quarter overall (total starts fell 16%q/q saar in 2Q on top of a 19% decline in the prior quarter, while permits dropped 22% in 2Q after a 1% drop in 1Q), pointing to a near-term drag on residential investment. Mortgage rates have drifted slightly lower in recent weeks, which will be supportive of housing, although the decline is still relatively small. Weekly mortgage purchase applications are largely moving sideways, while the NAHB housing market index for July continued to point to softness in the single-family space.

## A good run for manufacturing

The June industrial production report was solid, with total IP rising 0.6%m/m and the manufacturing group rising 0.4%m/ m (0.3%m/m ex motor vehicles) to the highest level since late 2022, inclusive of some upward revisions to the prior two months. For the quarter, manufacturing rose 3.6%q/q saar, the best gain in just over three years. Motor vehicle and parts industry output was up 13%q/q saar, but even excluding motor vehicles the 2.6% gain was the largest increase since the start of 2022 (Figure 3). As we discuss in this week’s Focus, the rebound in motor vehicle output is helping keep on lid on core goods inflation.

Figure 3: Manufacturing output Index, sa, 4Q19=100
![](images/70ae86fbf50523e75394958a2132b8b8bcf0f82bb426aea92596c66cf9c6856b.jpg)

The IP report gave a more positive message than has come from some of the business surveys: for example, the output index in the ISM manufacturing survey and the weighted composite of production/shipments from the regional Fed surveys had shown net decreases for June, though the output index in the S&P Global PMI had been running a bit better.

## Claims not sending the “all clear”

Initial jobless claims in the week ending July 13 spiked up to 243k from 223k the prior week, matching the 2024 high from the week of June 8. The four-week average of claims edged up to 235k from 234k, which is still down a bit from the recent high of 239k at the end of June, but is up materially from the roughly 210-215k range that was seen for much of the year through April. One item that may temper the negative interpretation of last week’s jump in initial claims is the effect of Hurricane Beryl, which made landfall in Texas on July 8 as a Category 1 hurricane, after which it was downgraded to lesser storm designations as it moved up across the Midwest and Northeast.

Perhaps more notable, continuing claims in the week ending July 6 rose to 1.867mm from 1.847mm the prior week, and are more convincingly breaking out of their range during the past year: prior to the rise over the last month they had not been higher than 1.829mm. The increase should be at least partly related to the larger inflow of new claimants, though changes in initial and continuing claims don’t always align. The further leg up in continuing claims bears watching as it may point to greater cooling in the labor market.

In addition to GDP and core PCE, next week we will get the flash PMIs for July, along with June data on home sales, durable goods, inventories, and trade. The flash PMIs will provide a timely reading on business activity in the manufacturing and services sectors. Existing home sales should remain depressed given the signal from pending home sales, while new home sales could recover some of their big drop in May.

## Data releases and forecasts

Sources for all: ADP/Moody’s Analytics, BEA, BLS, Census Bureau, Conference Board, Department of Labor, Federal Reserve Board, ISM, J.P. Morgan forecasts, NAHB, NAR, NFIB, NY Fed, Philadelphia Fed, S&P Global, Standard & Poor’s, University of Michigan, US Treasury

<table><tr><td>Tue Jul 23 10:00am</td><td>Existing home sales</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td></tr><tr><td>Total (mn, saar)</td><td>4.22</td><td>4.14</td><td>4.11</td><td>3.95</td></tr><tr><td>%m/m</td><td>-3.7</td><td>-1.9</td><td>-0.7</td><td>-3.9</td></tr><tr><td>%oya nsa</td><td>-9.5</td><td>6.8</td><td>-1.0</td><td>-3.9</td></tr><tr><td>Months&#x27; supply (nsa)</td><td>3.2</td><td>3.5</td><td>3.7</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Single-family</td><td>3.0</td><td>3.3</td><td>3.6</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Median price (%oya)</td><td>4.7</td><td>5.4</td><td>14.7</td><td></td></tr></table>

Existing home sales likely declined around 4%m/m in June to a 3.95mm annualized pace, putting them back nearer their cycle low of 3.85mm. Pending home sales from the National Association of Realtors, which lead existing home sales by 1-2 months, hit a new low in May after declining 8%m/m in April and another 2% in May. Other measures of pending home sales from Zillow and Redfin have also been weak. Meanwhile, mortgage purchase applications have largely been trending sideways at low levels. While they rose some in June they were down in each of the prior two months, which is more relevant for home sales closed in June.

We project that the manufacturing PMI slightly improved in July, rising to 52.0 from 51.6 in June. The PMI composite has been running somewhat above the ISM manufacturing composite, with the latter at 48.5 in June. However, a gap of this size is not unusual within the last year. Moreover, the PMIweighted composites for the Empire and Philadelphia Fed manufacturing surveys on net improved in July. While the Empire PMI composite did slide a bit to 48.6 from 49.1, the Philadelphia Fed composite surged from 47.5 to 58.3.

Wed S&P Global services PMI (US flash)
<table><tr><td>Jul 24</td><td>Index, sa</td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td rowspan="3">9:45am</td><td></td><td>Jul 23 52.3</td><td>Jun 24 55.3</td><td>Jul 24</td></tr><tr><td>Business activity</td><td>50.9</td><td>52.3</td><td>54.0</td></tr><tr><td>Employment</td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr></table>

The services PMI business activity index may have pulled back slightly in July to a still decent reading of 54.0 compared to 55.3 in July. Generally this index runs lower than the ISM services business activity index, though in both April and June the PMI index was above the ISM index, owing to low ISM readings in both months, combined with an uptick in the PMI index for May and June. This suggests some downside pressure on the PMI index, and/or some upside for the

ISM index. In terms of other service sector surveys, there is little available data from the regional Fed’s out yet for July, with the exception of the NY Fed’s Business Leaders Survey. The business activity and business climate measures in that report both maintained similar values between June and July.

Although the business activity index is not a composite of sub-indexes, other key indexes in the report have been moving closely with it. The new business index usually maintains a very tight relationship with general activity, and this index mimicked the upward move in general activity in May and June. Employment usually has a looser relationship, but it also increased over the last two months, rebounding from what appears to be a temporary dip in April.

<table><tr><td colspan="5">Wed New home sales</td></tr><tr><td>Jul 24 10:00am</td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td></tr><tr><td>Total (000s,saar)</td><td>684</td><td>698</td><td>619</td><td>630</td></tr><tr><td>%m/m</td><td>6.4</td><td>2.0</td><td>-11.3</td><td>1.8</td></tr><tr><td>%oya nsa</td><td>4.8</td><td>3.3</td><td>-16.4</td><td>-5.4</td></tr><tr><td>Months&#x27; supply</td><td>8.2</td><td>8.1</td><td>9.3</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Median price (%oya)</td><td>-0.5</td><td>0.2</td><td>-0.9</td><td></td></tr></table>

New single-family home sales plunged 11%m/m in May and we think there is a bit of room for them to recover in June. Looking past some of the month-to-month volatility new home sales have been sideways to slightly down over the last year and are currently running a bit weaker than they were pre-COVID, though continuing to hold in much better than existing home sales.

The NAHB survey’s index of current sales has been trending down recently, dipping from a recent high of 57 in April to 48 in June and 47 in July, though like sales the index has been fluctuating within a range over the last year. Mortgage purchase applications declined in the couple months prior to June, though these also have been mostly moving sideways at a very low level (most purchase applications will still be for the larger existing home market).

Beyond the sales figures the inventory data will be useful to watch. The inventory of homes for sale is at a cycle high and is the highest since 2008. Builders have recently been trimming starts which should help to stabilize inventory over time. It’s also possible the higher inventory will put some downward pressure on prices. After surging post-COVID the median sale price of new homes has remained fairly stable since the start of 2023.

Payroll survey week</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td colspan="7"></td></tr></table>

We look for initial jobless claims to retreat a bit to 235k in the week ending July 20 from 243k in the prior week, which would maintain the four-week average at the moderately elevated level of 235k. Jobless claims surprised to the upside last week, though some of the increase may have been related to the effects of Hurricane Beryl. However, Beryl would not have impacted the latest continuing claims figures, which are available through the week of July 6. Continuing claims had increased again to a new recent high. While the latest fourweek average is only about 60k higher than what it had been in the 12 months to the start of June, the upward trend is noticeable and bears watching. Keep in mind though that even in relatively stable economic environments we have seen larger changes in continuing claims. For example, the four-week average rose over 200k from mid 2018 to early 2019, and again from the fall of 2019 to early 2020 (pre-COVID).

<table><tr><td rowspan="8">Thu Jul 25 %m/m, sa 8:30am</td><td colspan="5">Durable goods</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td></tr><tr><td>New orders</td><td>0.8</td><td>0.2</td><td>0.1</td><td>-0.4</td></tr><tr><td>Ex transportation</td><td>-0.1</td><td>0.4</td><td>-0.1</td><td>-0.5</td></tr><tr><td>Nondef cap. gds ex air</td><td>-0.2</td><td>0.2</td><td>-0.6</td><td>-0.2</td></tr><tr><td>Shipments</td><td>0.1</td><td>1.1</td><td>-0.4</td><td>0.3</td></tr><tr><td>Nondef cap. gds ex air</td><td>-0.3</td><td>0.3</td><td>-0.6</td><td>-0.6</td></tr><tr><td>Inventories</td><td>0.0</td><td>0.1</td><td>0.3</td><td></td></tr></table>

Durable goods orders likely declined last month while shipments increased, although we anticipate that both orders and shipments fell for the important core capital goods segment (nondefense ex-aircraft). Growth in core capital goods orders shipments has been quite weak over the last year, and other leading indicators of these measures are soft. In particular, business equipment output in the industrial production report has been trending down since late 2022 and declined 0.4%m/ m in June, while capex expectations in the regional Fed surveys have been pointing to relatively little change in capital goods output. That being said, the story of domestic final demand for capital goods is somewhat better, owing to

imports. A simple measure of domestic absorption of capital goods output is core capital goods shipments less exports of nondefense ex-aircraft capital goods plus imports of the same. This measure rose 7.4% 3m/3m saar in May even as core capital goods declined shipments declined 1.6%.

Outside of core capital goods, we expect a positive contribution from motor vehicle output based on the industrial production report, and Boeing data suggest a gain in civilian aircraft shipments and a decline in orders. Both orders and shipments of goods ex transportation, capital goods, and defense have been range-bound over the last year and may not have much of a contribution. For defense, the upward drift in shipments could continue, but the volatile orders group could decline after two months of sizable gains.

Thu Gross domestic product Jul 25 %ch, q/q, saar, real, unless noted 8:30am

<table><tr><td></td><td>3Q23</td><td>4Q23</td><td>1Q24</td><td>2Q24</td></tr><tr><td>Real GDP</td><td>4.9</td><td>3.4</td><td>1.4</td><td>2.0</td></tr><tr><td>Final Sales</td><td>3.6</td><td>3.9</td><td>1.8</td><td>1.2</td></tr><tr><td>Domestic final sales</td><td>3.5</td><td>3.6</td><td>2.4</td><td>2.5</td></tr><tr><td>Consumption</td><td>3.1</td><td>3.3</td><td>1.5</td><td>2.0</td></tr><tr><td>Equipment</td><td>-4.4</td><td>-1.1</td><td>1.6</td><td>8.0</td></tr><tr><td>Intellectual property</td><td>1.8</td><td>4.3</td><td>7.7</td><td>6.0</td></tr><tr><td>Nonres. structures</td><td>11.2</td><td>10.9</td><td>3.4</td><td>-2.0</td></tr><tr><td>Residential investment</td><td>6.7</td><td>2.8</td><td>16.0</td><td>-0.5</td></tr><tr><td>Government</td><td>5.8</td><td>4.6</td><td>1.8</td><td>3.0</td></tr><tr><td>Exports</td><td>5.4</td><td>5.1</td><td>1.6</td><td>-1.7</td></tr><tr><td>Imports</td><td>4.2</td><td>2.2</td><td>6.1</td><td>8.0</td></tr><tr><td>Inventories (ch, $bn)</td><td>77.8</td><td>54.9</td><td>28.6</td><td>74.2</td></tr><tr><td>Net exports (pct.pt.contr.)</td><td>0.0</td><td>0.2</td><td>-0.6</td><td>-1.4</td></tr><tr><td>Inventories (pct.pt.contr.)</td><td>1.3</td><td>-0.5</td><td>-0.4</td><td>0.8</td></tr><tr><td>Core PCE price index</td><td>2.0</td><td>2.0</td><td>3.7</td><td>2.8</td></tr><tr><td>(%oya)</td><td>3.8</td><td>3.2</td><td>2.9</td><td>2.7</td></tr><tr><td>GDP chain price index (%oya)</td><td>3.3 3.2</td><td>1.6 2.6</td><td>3.1 2.4</td><td>2.1 2.5</td></tr></table>

We look for real GDP to have increased 2.0%q/q, saar in 2Q. While a step down from the very rapid 4.2% pace in 2H23, this is an improvement on the 1.4% gain in 1Q, driven by stronger growth in consumption, equipment investment, inventory building, and government spending. With more inventory accumulation the acceleration in domestic final sales is not as apparent as for overall GDP: domestic final sales should increase 2.5% in 2Q vs. 2.4% in 1Q.

Based on the monthly PCE data and other available information for June we think that real consumer spending growth picked up, increasing 2.0% in the quarter after growing 1.5% the prior quarter, with solid momentum going into the end of the quarter. Consumption was supported by gains in real disposable income, which we estimate rose 1.8%.

On the investment front, there should be a split between declines in structures spending and more rapid growth in equipment/IPP. We expect that both nonresidential and residential structures investment declined in the quarter after growing in 1Q, and for residential the drag is likely to get worse in the coming quarters given the recent trajectory of housing starts and declining number of units under construction. For equipment spending, we expect reasonably strong growth stemming from vehicles, aircraft, and computers, but with roughly no contribution from other capital goods.

After subtracting from growth in 1Q, more rapid inventory building should make a positive contribution to GDP in 2Q, though this will be partially offset by a widening trade deficit. Finally, we look for stronger growth in government spending relative to 1Q, though the pace should be slower than in 2H23.

The advance economic indicators report will contain the first print for the goods trade balance for June along with wholesale and retail inventories. For trade we look for the nominal goods deficit to have narrowed very slightly. The combination of port traffic data and export/import prices point to small gains in exports and imports, with exports marginally outpacing imports.

<table><tr><td rowspan="3">Thu Jul 25 8:30am</td><td colspan="5">Business inventories (adv.)</td></tr><tr><td>%m/m, sa, unless noted</td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td></tr><tr><td>Wholesale</td><td>-0.5</td><td>0.2</td><td>0.6</td><td>0.5</td></tr><tr><td>Retail inventories</td><td></td><td>0.1</td><td>0.8</td><td>0.6</td><td>0.8</td></tr><tr><td>Ex autos</td><td></td><td>-0.4</td><td>0.2</td><td>0.0</td><td>0.2</td></tr><tr><td>Autos</td><td></td><td>1.3</td><td>1.9</td><td>2.0</td><td>2.0</td></tr></table>

For inventories, we expect a moderate 0.5%m/m gain in wholesale inventories and a close to 1% increase in retail inventories, with most of retail coming from motor vehicles. Wholesale inventories have been mostly flat over the last year though with a little bit of a pickup in the prior couple months. Retail inventories meanwhile have been mostly stable ex motor vehicles over the last six months, while motor vehicle inventories have been steadily rebounding from their post-COVID lows. Motor vehicle inventories probably kept rising in June, based on industry data from Wards.

<table><tr><td rowspan="7">Fri Jul 26 8:30am</td><td colspan="5">Personal income</td></tr><tr><td>%m/m, sa, unless noted</td><td>Mar</td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td></tr><tr><td>Personal income</td><td>0.5</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.5</td><td>0.3</td></tr><tr><td>Wages &amp; salaries</td><td>0.6</td><td>0.2</td><td>0.7</td><td>0.4</td></tr><tr><td>Consumption</td><td>0.7</td><td>0.1</td><td>0.2</td><td>0.3</td></tr><tr><td>Real consumption</td><td>0.3</td><td>-0.1</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.2</td></tr><tr><td>PCE price index</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.0</td><td>0.1</td></tr><tr><td>Core</td><td>0.33</td><td>0.26</td><td>0.08</td><td>0.18</td></tr><tr><td>Mkt-Based Core</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.2</td><td>0.2</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Core (%oya)</td><td>2.8</td><td>2.8</td><td>2.6</td><td>2.6</td></tr><tr><td>Mkt-Based Core (%oya)</td><td>2.6</td><td>2.5</td><td>2.4</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Saving rate</td><td>3.5</td><td>3.7</td><td>3.9</td><td>3.9</td></tr></table>

Based on already released data from the CPI, PPI, and import price reports we project that the core PCE deflator rose 0.2%m/m (0.18%) in June, which will maintain the over-yearago pace at 2.6%. The headline PCE deflator should rise 0.1%m/m. The latest core PCE reading would be an acceleration from the 0.08%m/m pace seen in May, but would still represent another month of slower inflation than was seen in the elevated first quarter. Recent commentary from Fed speakers suggest they see such readings as broadly favorable and consistent with a first cut in interest rates later this year.

For consumption, we look for total real spending to have risen 0.2%m/m, which along with some expected upward revisions to earlier months would leave total consumer spending for 2Q up roughly 2%q/q saar. The retail sales data for June was decent, and we expect a positive contribution from utilities spending and other services, offset to some degree by a decline in spending on vehicles.

Real disposable income is set to slow a bit in June after a strong 0.5%m/m increase in May, but our forecasted 0.3%m/ m gain is still quite decent. Nominal wages and salaries should increase around 0.4%m/m, consistent with the June increase in aggregate weekly payrolls (the product of hourly earnings, weekly hours, and employment) for private industry workers.

<table><tr><td colspan="5">Fri Consumer sentiment Jul 26</td></tr><tr><td colspan="3">10:00am</td><td>Pre</td><td>Fin</td></tr><tr><td>Univ. of Mich. Index (nsa)</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td><td>Jul</td><td>Jul</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>69.1</td><td>68.2</td><td>66.0</td><td>66.0</td></tr><tr><td>Current conditions</td><td>69.6 68.8</td><td>65.9 69.6</td><td>64.1 67.2</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Expectations</td><td></td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Inflation expectations Short-term</td><td>3.0</td><td>3.0</td><td>2.9</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Long term</td><td>0.0</td><td>0.0</td><td>2.9</td><td></td></tr></table>

We don’t expect that the University of Michigan consumer sentiment index changed materially between the final and preliminary July reports after having declined for the fourth consecutive month in early July. Consumer sentiment in this report has been quite depressed relative to measures of real activity and its own history. Although one-year and five-year inflation expectations among respondents have come down and are no longer particularly elevated, the Michigan press release has noted that “nearly half” of panelists are concerned about high prices, likely a reflection of the step up in the price level that has already occurred.

The preliminary survey took place about the first presidential debate, although the press release noted that there was “little evidence” it had changed economic views. Major political headlines since then could cause some additional volatility in sentiment.

In April to July the Michigan survey has been switching from telephone surveys to web surveys. Web respondents tend to give more negative responses, and the survey estimates that the switch will decrease the headline index by around 6.5 points between March and July, with the effect more concentrated in assessments of current conditions.

## Empire State survey (Jul 15)

<table><tr><td>musion iniuiccs, sa</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td><td>Jul</td></tr><tr><td>General bus. conditions</td><td>-15.6</td><td>-6.0</td><td>4.0 -6.6</td></tr><tr><td>New orders</td><td>-16.5</td><td>-1.0</td><td>-0.6</td></tr><tr><td>Shipments</td><td>-1.2</td><td>3.3</td><td>3.9</td></tr><tr><td>Unfilled orders</td><td>-8.1</td><td>1.0</td><td>-11.2</td></tr><tr><td>Prices paid</td><td>28.3</td><td>24.5</td><td>26.5</td></tr><tr><td>Prices received</td><td>14.1</td><td>7.1</td><td>6.1</td></tr><tr><td>Composite</td><td>46.9</td><td>49.0</td><td>48.0</td></tr></table>

The headline measure of the Empire State manufacturing survey edged down to -6.6 in July, marginally below the -6.0 recorded in June. This survey has been very volatile since the start of the pandemic but has generally remained soft over the past year. The implied July reading on the headline manufacturing ISM from the report slid to 48.0, from 49.1 in June. Within the Empire State survey, shipments, new orders, and employment all improved slightly on the month, whereas the delivery time and inventory components posted larger declines. (The work week, not part of the implied ISM, improved more meaningfully in July to an effectively neutral reading of -0.1.) While the small improvements in the shipments and new orders components are encouraging for manufacturing activity, it’s worth noting that production in the manufacturing ISM has been trending lower in recent months and stood at 48.5 in June. The sharper drop in inventories also should be encouraging for production, although the six-month ahead expectations for shipments moved down somewhat as well, hinting at concerns that future demand may be less

strong than previously thought. There also was a slight tick up in the six-month ahead expectation for capital expenditures, although at 3.1 this reading is near its lowest level outside of prior recessions. All told, the July Empire State survey still points to lingering softness in the manufacturing sector.

## Retail sales (Jul 16)

<table><tr><td colspan="7">%m/m, sa</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Apr</td><td></td><td>May</td><td></td><td>Jun</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Total</td><td>-0.2</td><td></td><td>0.1</td><td>0.3</td><td>05</td><td>0.0</td></tr><tr><td>Ex autos</td><td>-0.1</td><td>0.1</td><td>-0.1</td><td>0.1</td><td>申</td><td>0.4</td></tr><tr><td>Ex autos and gas</td><td>-0.3</td><td>-0.1</td><td>-0.1</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.3</td><td>0.8</td></tr><tr><td>Building materials</td><td>-0.3</td><td>0.4</td><td>-0.8</td><td>-0.7</td><td>0.3</td><td>1.4</td></tr><tr><td>Control group1</td><td>-0.5</td><td>-0.3</td><td>0.4</td><td></td><td>03</td><td>0.9</td></tr><tr><td>Ex. autos and building mat.</td><td>-0.1</td><td>0.0</td><td>-0.0</td><td>0.2</td><td>申</td><td>0.4</td></tr></table>

1. Total ex. gasoline, automotive dealers, building materials, and food serv.

Retail sales were stronger than expected in June, the main reason we upgraded our forecast for 2Q real consumer spending to 2.0% from 1.7% this week. Total nominal retail sales and food services spending was unchanged in the month, dragged down by declines for motor vehicles and gas stations, but excluding those categories spending rose a solid 0.8%m/ m. The control category (ex auto, gas, building materials, and food services) also increased 0.9%m/m compared to expectations for a 0.2-0.3% gain, and there were upward revisions to May and April. The latest number should ease concerns about an abrupt loss of momentum in consumer spending and further temper any notion that the Fed has to rush into the first rate cut.

Looking across some of the other categories of spending, food services rose 0.3%m/m and the prior month’s 0.4% drop was revised to a 0.4% increase. Spending at restaurants had been quite soft from December through March but has risen in each of the last three months. Sales at building material stores, which have been hampered by the slowdown in the housing market, increased a decent 1.4%m/m. Sales were also up for most categories of spending within the control group.

## Import prices (Jul 16)

<table><tr><td rowspan="2">%m/m, nsa, unless noted</td><td colspan="5"></td><td rowspan="2"></td></tr><tr><td>Apr</td><td></td><td>May</td><td></td><td>Jun</td></tr><tr><td>Import prices</td><td>0.9</td><td>1.0</td><td>-0.4</td><td>-0.2</td><td>0.0</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td>1.1</td><td></td><td>1.1</td><td>1.4</td><td>13</td><td>1.6</td></tr><tr><td>Ex-fuel import prices</td><td>0.7</td><td></td><td>-0.3</td><td></td><td>04</td><td>0.2</td></tr><tr><td>%oya</td><td>-0.8</td><td>0.9</td><td>0.5</td><td></td><td>0.9</td><td>1.0</td></tr></table>

The import price index was unchanged in June, with the May value revised to be slightly less negative (-0.2%) than originally reported. Imported petroleum prices dropped -1.2% on the month, leaving nonfuel import prices up 0.2% (0.156% to three decimals) in June. These readings were above consensus expectations but broadly aligned with our forecasts. Moreover, the data show a continued upward climb of the year-ago inflation rates for the import price index: headline now stands at 1.6% while nonfuels is at 1.0%oya. Both of

these values represent their highest readings since December 2022. Recent increases in shipping costs may be bolstering import prices in the near-term, but the appreciation of the US dollar since the start of this year (up about 3.8% since end-2023 after some modest recent weakening) should eventually push import prices back down.

Most of the detailed components of the import price index were little changed on the month. June import prices for autos ticked up 0.1% while those for consumer goods ex autos ticked down -0.1%. Import prices for capital goods were unchanged on the month. In year-ago terms, imported autos prices rose about 2.5% whereas other imported consumer goods have seen a milder 0.6%oya pace; both modestly increased in June. Imported capital goods have been in slight deflationary territory since the end of last year, and for June were running at a -0.2%oya pace.

The NAHB housing market index was little changed in July, falling to 42 from 43 in June. It is down from a recent high of 51 in March/April, but since late 2022 the index has been oscillating within a range that is considerably depressed relative to pre-COVID values, consistent with the drag on the housing market from high mortgage rates. New home sales through May have been holding in better than existing home sales, but single-family home inventories have been increasing and builders have recently trimmed starts. The latest homebuilder survey, along with the soft weekly data on mortgage purchase applications, suggests that this dynamic could be continuing to play out.

<table><tr><td rowspan="2">Million units, saar</td><td colspan="6"></td></tr><tr><td>Apr</td><td></td><td>May</td><td></td><td>Jun</td><td>1.35</td></tr><tr><td>Starts</td><td>1.35</td><td>1.38</td><td>1.28</td><td>1.31</td><td>1.25</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Single-family starts</td><td>1.04</td><td></td><td>0.98</td><td>1.00</td><td>0.96</td><td>0.98</td></tr><tr><td>Multifamily starts</td><td>0.32</td><td>0.34</td><td>0.30</td><td>0.31</td><td>0.29</td><td>0.37</td></tr><tr><td>Permits</td><td>1.44</td><td></td><td>1.40</td><td></td><td>140</td><td>1.45</td></tr></table>

Housing starts and permits rebounded in June, led by the volatile multifamily segment, while single-family activity continued to moderate. Single-family construction is likely pulling back temporarily in response to the recent rise in inventories, while multifamily construction was coming off a low base as builders had already sharply cut starts in prior months as they contend with an elevated pace of new deliveries. Both starts and permits are down substantially in the second quarter overall, pointing to a near-term drag on residential investment. Mortgage rates have drifted slightly lower in recent weeks which will be supportive of housing, though the decline is still relatively small and weekly mortgage purchase applications are largely moving sideways, while the NAHB housing market index for July continued to point to softness in the single-family space.

Total starts rose 3.0%m/m in June to a 1.353mm saar pace, with single-family starts declining 2.2% but multifamily starts up 19.6%. Permits rose 3.4%m/m to 1.446mm saar, with single-family declining 2.3% and multifamily rising 15.6%. Taking into account some upward revisions to starts in the prior couple months, total starts fell 16%q/q saar in 2Q on top of a 19% decline in the prior quarter, while permits dropped 22% in 2Q after a 1% drop in 1Q.

The latest monthly print leaves single-family starts down 14% from their recent high in February. The new single-family home sales report for May had shown that inventories were at a cycle-high that month and up 13%oya, so it is not surprising that builders are adjusting down starts to control supply. Multifamily builders had already made more of that adjustment over the last year and the June jump in multifamily starts leaves starts at a level not that different from pre-COVID rates, though down substantially from the elevated levels that persisted through the middle of last year. Because of the delay between starts and completions the new supply of multifamily units remains elevated, and in June it rose 42%oya to the highest level since the mid-1980s. Fortunately this comes against the backdrop of rental vacancy rates that, while up a percentage point from their recent post-COVID lows, are still low in comparison to recent decades.
