The June industrial production report was solid, with total IP rising 0.6%m/m and the manufacturing group rising 0.4%m/ m (0.3%m/m ex motor vehicles) to the highest level since late 2022, inclusive of some upward revisions to the prior two months. This is a more positive message than has come from

Murat Tasci (1-212) 622-0288murat.tasci@jpmchase.comAbiel Reinhart (1-212) 270 4058abiel.reinhart@jpmchase.com

some of the business surveys: for example, the output index in the ISM manufacturing survey and the weighted composite of production/shipments from the regional Fed surveys had shown net decreases for June, though the output index in the S&P PMI had been running a bit better.

For the quarter, manufacturing rose 3.6%q/q saar, the best gain in just over three years. Motor vehicle and parts industry output was up 13%q/q saar, but even excluding motor vehicles the 2.6% gain was the largest increase since the start of 2022. Moreover, computers and peripheral equipment, which feed into equipment investment in the GDP report, were up 3.6%q/q saar, which, while not a particularly large rise for this category, comes after two straight quarterly declines (although the segment did fall 2.5%m/m). Elsewhere the business equipment category ex motor vehicles and high-tech continued to show softness, declining 0.7%m/m and 1.3%q/q saar for 2Q. This group has been falling slowly since late 2022.

Outside the manufacturing sector, mining was up a slight 0.3%m/m while utilities, which are correlated with consumer utilities spending, increased 2.8%. Utilities are up 12%q/q saar for 2Q, a fairly strong quarterly increase. Mining had rebounded sharply post COVID but peaked last fall near its pre-pandemic level, and since then it has been mostly edging down.

## Philadelphia Fed survey (Jul 18)

<table><tr><td>Difiusion indices, sa</td><td>May</td><td>Jun</td><td>Jul</td></tr><tr><td>General bus. Conditions</td><td>4.5</td><td>1.3</td><td>1 13.9</td></tr><tr><td>New orders</td><td>-7.9</td><td>-2.2</td><td>20.7</td></tr><tr><td>Shipments</td><td>-1.2</td><td>-7.2</td><td>27.8</td></tr><tr><td>Inventories</td><td>-10.1</td><td>-6.4</td><td>-9.4</td></tr><tr><td>Prices paid</td><td>18.7</td><td>22.5</td><td>19.8</td></tr><tr><td>Prices received</td><td>6.6</td><td>13.7</td><td>24.2</td></tr><tr><td>Composite</td><td>47.2</td><td>47.2</td><td>56.3</td></tr></table>

The Philly Fed manufacturing survey was strong in July. The general business conditions index jumped to 13.9 from 1.3 in June. While the headline was higher in April (15.5), the components were stronger, and the ISM-weighted composite increased from 47.2 in June to 56.3 in July, the best reading since May 2022. This was driven by increases in most major components, including new orders (from -2.2 to 20.7), shipments (from -7.2 to 27.8) , and employment (from -2.5 to 15.2). Recall though that the Empire manufacturing survey, released earlier in the month, was more downbeat: its ISMweighted composite had declined from 49.1 to 48.0.

On the pricing front there was a sizable rise in the prices received index, which jumped from 13.7 to 24.2, the highest reading since the start of 2023. Again this is a different message from the Empire survey, where the same index was only 6.1, near the low for the cycle.

While expectations for future business activity were strong, with the general conditions index for six months ahead (38.7) reversing some temporary June weakness (13.8), the capex plans index declined to 7.4 from 12.1. The capex index has been quite elevated through May and has moderated substantially in the last two months.

## Focus: Vehicle prices

The early stages of the recent inflation episode were dominated by a surge in core goods price inflation, which peaked at 12.5% in February 2022. At that time, vehicle prices accounted for 80% of the increase in core goods prices (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Core goods inflation %-pt. contribution to over year-ago CPI inflation

![](images/d8fe88d0bb8f665a36456a2c4a22ca1493b08a420ca8709b6fb94baf326c356e.jpg)

Recently, core goods prices have also led the overall core CPI disinflation, as core goods prices in June were down 1.7% over a year-ago, and vehicle prices accounted for 79% of the decline in core goods prices over the past year. So the core goods inflation story has been, and likely will continue to be, dominated by the outlook for vehicle prices.

Our auto industry analysts expect prices for new vehicles to decline 2.5% this year and look for used vehicle prices to decline 5%-10% (most automakers are projecting similar declines). If realized, then vehicle prices would decline 0.1%-0.2% per month on average in the second half, which would be above the first-half average readings but below the flat average outturns in 2023 (Figure 2).

Figure 2: Vehicle prices
![](images/bdc63ed6666eec3ae25e412f96cb5d555a8bd5d697bb8e85da46e9512e557ee9.jpg)

The forecast for further declines is predicated on improving supply against a backdrop of muted demand. Supply was severely curtailed in ’20-’22, most notably due to a shortage of automotive semiconductors. The production shortfall pushed vehicle inventories to an all-time low in late ’21. As the chip bottleneck has been unclogged inventories have

recovered to closer to normal levels (Figure 3).

![](images/687e79d2fb8e43235d5ec8f38f2e4d6fb936482f304fbd97cc3d3add722a0230.jpg)

Vehicle production continues to increase: the 11.75 million annual rate of vehicle assemblies in June was the highest in almost four years. More of those vehicles are continuing to go into inventory on dealer lots as demand has remained contained.

High prices and high interest rates have combined to sour consumer moods on car buying, as reflected in the University of Michigan survey of consumers car buying attitudes (Figure 4).

Figure 4: Car buying conditions, balance on "good time"
![](images/9e6976d5a8a15ab61853d8abec3d762dee9d41378b117788364dec7c9fb19c32.jpg)
About 80% of new vehicle purchases are financed, with an average loan length of a little over five years. The presumptive Fed rate cuts should boost car buying attitudes, but any recovery in demand is likely to occur against a more normal inventory backdrop.

## Euro Area

• ECB kept rates on hold this week

• But, barring large data surprises, a cut in September remains on track

• Sequential core price momentum slowed in June

• Next week: PMI to recover modestly

The ECB kept rates on hold this week and Lagarde said that a decision in September was “wide open”. But, in our view, these statements do not mean that the decision in September will necessarily be close. In particular, the ECB said that the “incoming information broadly supports our previous assessment of the medium-term inflation outlook”. This outlook leans heavily on the staff’s June projections, which have factored in further rate cuts via the market curve from mid-May. As a result, we regard this week’s rhetoric as consistent with another rate cut in September and therefore stick to our existing rate call, which has cuts in September, December and five more next year.

The opening policy statement noted that domestic price pressures “are still high”, services inflation is “elevated” and headline inflation “is likely to remain above our target well into next year”. But, these statements largely describe what was already in the staff’s June forecast. During the press conference, Lagarde commented more extensively on wages, which are high right now but are still expected to slow next year and even more in 2026. She described the likely wage developments in 2H25 and early 2026 as consistent with inflation being at target. Lagarde also took some comfort from the unit profits data, which suggest a further absorption of wage pressures that still reflect a catch-up to the past high inflation and larger one-off payments. Hence, overall, there is nothing in the recent inflation and wage data that seems to be challenging in a material way the broader disinflation narrative. We were slightly surprised however that the decline in June was not mentioned more explicitly. In any case, with two more PMI reports and two more inflation reports to come before the September meeting, that policy decision can still be affected, but, for now, we would conclude that a September rate cut remains firmly on the agenda.

## A smaller core price gain in June

The main focus in this week’s final HICP print for June was whether the details would cast some doubt on the smaller increase in core prices that had been reported in the flash release. That was not the case. The core price increase was revised up slightly to 0.23%m/m, sa, due to services. But, this was still softer than the 0.30%m/m, sa, average seen between January and May (Figure 1). This is encouraging and would be consistent with earlier price pressures having been quite concentrated in a relatively narrow set of core categories, such as insurance.

Figure 1: Euro area core HICP prices %m/m, sa
![](images/2e1d233bf15bf45d4403707eae508127605b8de65a7abe591c035251b6b103e0.jpg)

Of course, a core price gain of 0.23%m/m, sa, is still above target as it equates to a 2.8%ar. As a result, a further slowing will need to come through. Consistent with this, there is not yet much slowing visible in the smoother %3m, saar, momentum in terms of core inflation or its two main components (Figure 2). Hence, at this stage, core goods momentum remains close to zero, despite a slightly firmer increase in prices in June, while services momentum remains at around a 5%ar, despite a smaller price gain of 0.30%m/m, sa, in June.

Figure 2: Euro area core inflation
![](images/755cef4ab4196a711595dcf361d9613dbf3d9edb058314f5f7ac370e53edb283.jpg)

However, the details suggest that a significant part of the services momentum has come from insurance prices, which have accelerated sharply this year (Figure 3). With a weight of 2% in the HICP, 3% in core and 4% in services, they have contributed significantly to the inflation pressures seen so far this year. Significant pressure is also still coming from tourism-related items, although airfares have declined recently (Figure 4). The main concern is still that some of these pressures, together with those seen in some other items, still reflect wage-related pressures that will fade more slowly. However, our reading of the incoming news on wages is similar to that of the ECB, which is that high wage growth at present still reflects a catch-up with the high inflation of the past two years and that the forward-looking implications of new deals point to a significant slowing next year. In any case, the June

HICP is only a first step in a more moderate direction and will need to be validated by a gradual further slowing in the next few releases.

Figure 3: Euro area HICP - insurance prices
![](images/3cc30e2f112a6e65bc8aedc9516e2c8627c09275f37fa8b719d62799fc50da16.jpg)

Figure 4: Euro area tourism inflation
![](images/f8fcfee62ed74d9b210f4951ee6f5d3757a1df2d33564961fd4e349f2cc8e5dd.jpg)

## Bank lending survey shows improvement

The ECB Bank Lending Survey showed a further improvement. The net percentage on lending standards to households and nonfinancial corporates remained at zero, which points to stable lending standards (or arguably even a slight easing), while banks reported a first increase in loan demand in two years (Figure 5). The latter reflects a solid increase in demand for mortgages and consumer credit, while loan demand from nonfinancial corporates contracted at a much more moderate pace. These changes reflected improved prospects for the housing market and a smaller contraction in companies’ demand for loans to finance capital spending. The forwardlooking questions for the next three months showed a continuation of these improved trends. In this context, we note that the survey period came after the first round of the French election and that France does not stand out on the downside in terms of the main results. On its own, this survey suggests that the strength of monetary policy transmission is easing, although the June composite PMI has dampened that message by showing a softening in overall growth momentum.

Figure 5: ECB bank lending survey: NFCs and households Net %, higher values indicate tighter loan supply and higher loan demand
![](images/d50fd159b909de39a249378812f100da1eecd72502421e461156d517323dcedb.jpg)

## Euro area IP disappointed in May

Euro area IP fell 0.6%m/m in May in total and by 0.8%/m in manufacturing. The very volatile and distorted Irish data actually lifted the Euro area outturn in May with gains of around 6%m/m. Excluding Ireland, Euro area IP fell 1.2%m/m in total and by 1.5%m/m in manufacturing (Figure 6). But, these larger declines were likely affected by the timing of holidays. This was mentioned explicitly by the French statistics office, while the profile of German car production in the VDA data also points in that direction. In particular, German car production jumped in April, fell sharply in May and recovered in June. Assuming that IP improves in June, it would track only a small decline in 2Q24. But, the bigger problem for the Euro area IP data is that the manufacturing PMI weakened substantially in June, pointing to ongoing contraction in an underlying sense. This remains harder to understand and suggests that any slight improvement in the actual IP data may be short-lived.

Figure 6: Euro area IP - manufacturing
![](images/ef7a0ef7a8dc88b12981ca7161ce399ee9090ad5a47859d027ec1361403c25f0.jpg)

## Next week: Looking for a better PMI

The focus in next week’s data will be on the flash PMI for July. Following the setback in June, which took its signal for GDP growth from 1.5%ar to 1.1%ar, we look for about a quarter of this decline to be reversed. It will be particularly important to see manufacturing improve as it accounted for most of the June weakness and is at a very low level.

Week of July 22-26

Output and surveys

<table><tr><td>Consumer confidence (prelim)</td><td></td><td></td><td>Jun</td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Apr</td><td>May</td><td></td><td>Jul</td></tr><tr><td>Tue Jul 23</td><td>Euro area (European Commission survey)</td><td></td><td></td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>% balance of responses</td><td></td><td>-14.0</td><td>-13.5</td></tr><tr><td>4:00pm</td><td>Consumer confidence</td><td>-14.7</td><td>-14.3</td><td></td></tr></table>

While the Euro area composite PMI declined in June, consumer confidence continued to improve. This likely reflects the resilience in labor markets and that real incomes are growing solidly at present. The latter reflects the constellation of solid gains in nominal wages and lower headline inflation. As both of these factors remain in place, we suspect that consumer confidence improved further in July.

The Euro area composite had improved through to May to a level consistent with 1.5%ar on GDP growth. It then fell very abruptly in June down to a level consistent with 1.1%ar GDP growth. That slowing was largely due to a sharp fall in manufacturing, which slipped deeper into contraction territory. Services also slowed but from a solid level. Unfortunately, it was unclear what drove the deterioration in June. There was a suggestion that the French election played a role, but this did not show up in the most obvious way in the survey. And other growth drivers are unlikely to have changed much.

For these reasons, we are inclined to think that the PMI will partially recover in July, reversing a quarter of June’s decline. We expect this to come entirely from manufacturing. We also expect other components to reverse some of June’s declines. In terms of the labor market, this would leave employment growth very resilient relative to the pace of GDP growth, implying ongoing weakness in labor productivity.

The German IFO deteriorated modestly in June, driven by lower business expectations. Interpreting these outcomes is made difficult by the fact that the German IFO has consistently underpredicted GDP growth over the past two years, while the PMI has tracked GDP outturns better than the IFO. The German PMI also fell in June, largely reflecting an abrupt relapse in manufacturing. We expect the PMI to improve in July and have therefore also pencilled in an increase for the IFO.

## Japan

• Underlying inflation inflecting higher through June

• Manufacturers more optimistic despite auto sector noise

• Export momentum firming

• Next week: July PMI turns up

Japan’s economy weathered multiple shocks through 1H24, with the manufacturing sector buffeted by a New Year earthquake and by two rounds of auto production suspensions on the back of safety testing scandals. These drags on factory output have created volatility not just in production, but also in exports and manufacturing employment, dampening sentiment. Volatility in Japan’s goods producing sector has combined with a lackluster trend in domestic demand, depressing overall GDP growth in 1Q.

This week’s data flow was relatively light, but indicators are sending encouraging signs of a stabilization in activity through midyear. We had seen the risk of auto production suspensions extending through the summer, cutting short a turn higher in manufacturing momentum in 2Q. But this week’s July Reuters Tankan survey suggested producers, including auto manufacturers, are turning more optimistic going into 3Q, while a regional tech cycle upswing is bolstering Japan’s electronics sector. Momentum in export growth also firmed to its fastest pace in almost a year through June, albeit gains are not yet broad-based.

A continued recovery in Japan’s manufacturing sector should combine with a firmer footing for domestic demand. As nominal wage growth continues to rise, real income growth is turning up even in the face of sticky underlying inflation. We expect household consumption to have stabilized this quarter, lifting overall GDP growth to an above-trend 1%-1.5%q/q saar pace. Alongside firming underlying inflation, this cyclical recovery should give the BoJ cover to press on with gradual policy rate normalization.

## June CPI suggests underlying inflation rise

Measures of underlying core inflation accelerated in the nationwide June CPI. The global standard measure of core prices rose 0.2%m/m sa, leaving core inflation at 1.9%oya and similarly up 1.9%3m/3m saar (Figure 1). This marks an acceleration over a flat monthly reading and 1.7%3m/3m saar pace in May. The BoJ’s measures of core inflation were mixed. A 3.0%m/m sa jump in energy prices pushed the official measure of core prices up a firm 0.4%m/m sa, leaving BoJ official core inflation at 2.6%oya, 10bp softer than consensus expectations. BoJ’s preferred measure of core (ex. fresh food and energy) rose 0.2%m/m sa and was up

2.2%oya, in line with expectations.

The message from the CPI data has been distorted recently by the expiration (and, from August, reintroduction) of energy subsidies, as well as changes in tuition fee subsidies. But we think the BoJ will take comfort from two signals in the data that suggest underlying inflation is firming.

First, core goods prices (which exclude the impact of volatile energy price changes and food) were up a firm 0.6%m/m sa in June, and accelerated 3.0%3m/3m saar, their fastest trend rise in almost a year (Figure 2). We suspect stickiness here reflects sustained passthrough from higher input costs that are being passed on to consumers, also reflected in the trend rise in the corporate goods price index from the early part of the year. Second, while services price momentum fell in June, this softening largely reflects the impact of government-administered prices. Prices of education (-1.1%oya), medical care (1.4%), and public transportation (1.9%) remained relatively subdued. By contrast, other services prices were much firmer. Culture and recreation prices were up 5.6%oya in June, while private transportation costs have been averaging above 3%oya through the year to date.

Figure 1: Japan Global standard core prices (ex. food and energy) % chg.
![](images/6139c413b520e86c32a4339192729f48a7e5e682f9beacfd32cc5654b652f551.jpg)

Figure 2: Japan Core goods prices, ex. food and energy % chg.
![](images/25d4aae6af6aacedc0a402868d8989f4c0f10576de1520d53974069e1cf80daa.jpg)

Our forecast anticipates a firming in underlying inflation after a transitory lull in early 2Q. Energy inflation likely will continue to rise sharply into July, which should feed into underlying inflation with a lag, before the reintroduction of subsidies later this summer. And, as services inflation continues to firm alongside rising wage growth, we expect the BoJ to signal rising confidence in achieving its 2% inflation target.

## Business sentiment edges higher

Sentiment among large manufacturers recovered in July. The Reuters Tankan survey of manufacturing sentiment rose 5pts to 11, reflecting rising optimism across almost all key manufacturing sectors. There were particularly strong rises among auto producers (+14pts) and electronics manufacturers (+9pts), as well as sectors including chemicals and oil refining. Alongside a tentative turn higher in confidence among SMEs reflected in last week’s Economy Watchers survey, large manufacturing firms appear to be benefitting from a gradual fading of drags on production (Figure 3).

Non-manufacturing sentiment fell 4pts to 27, but the balance of respondents reporting optimistic conditions overall remains above the level in the prior few months (Figure 4). Outlook indicators suggest broadly similar conditions ahead, as was also the case for manufacturers. That said, sentiment declines across services and construction sectors were broad-based, with particularly large drops in the wholesale and retail sector. Wholesale sentiment fell to 5, its lowest level since the mid-2021 pandemic shock.

Figure 3: Reuters Tankan large firms sentiment - Headline DI, level of conditions, boxes denote 3-mo outlook
![](images/3964c93a323b21109204e91bceaf2715fcd151cdd6cb98fbda65321ddb6f08d9.jpg)
Source: Reuters, J.P. Morgan

Figure 4: Japan Non-mfg. firms' business sentiment St. dev from 2Y trailing mean
![](images/16692b653050de8560a5297c07d819f4693a66076a685bc8a3b56a911a060a1e.jpg)

In this context, survey respondents in July reported sustained pressure on margins from currency depreciation, rising raw material costs, and a higher wage bill that is translating into continued cost pass-through into consumer prices. But manufacturers also cited the impact of softer Chinese demand that has seen raw material supplies diverted to Japan at lower costs; the impact of this trend on price inputs will bear watching.

## Export momentum recovering

Japan’s June real exports rose 1.6%m/m sa, leaving exports up 2.1%3m/3m saar, their fastest pace in 10 months. This was a stronger outcome than our expectation for a flat reading, though details were not particularly upbeat, with auto and electronics export volumes posting monthly declines. Real imports were up 3.5%m/m sa and accelerated 13.5%3m/3m saar. At face value, this suggests that net trade was a small drag on GDP growth last quarter. However, given very strong net tourism receipts through the quarter, we suspect services exports rose firmly in 2Q. We are thus leaving unchanged our forecast of a modest 0.6%-pt addition to 2Q GDP from net trade.

Despite the headline rise in exports, several key product categories were weak in June. We suspect most of the drag on external trade owes to a pause in activity in the auto sector with potentially negative spillovers into supply chains. Manufacturers’ projections were for a near-6% plunge in output in June, and this looks also to have affected producers’ ability to fill foreign orders last month. Real auto shipments fell 2.8%m/m sa, with particular weakness in Euro area-bound exports. Tech (-3.5%) and other capital goods (-0.3%) were also soft. These drags should fade through 3Q; we look to next week’s July flash PMI to confirm manufacturing activity is stabilizing.

## Canada

• Markets are nearly fully priced for a BoC rate cut next week

• While a very close call, we expect policy to stay on hold...

• … given just enough signs in the CPI and BOS to warrant a gradual and cautious approach to easing

• May retail sales disappointed but 2Q consumption growth tracking around 2.5%ar

The Bank of Canada holds its July policy meeting next week, and the decision looks to be a very close call. Markets are nearly fully priced for a rate cut, but the economic rationale is more nuanced. The job of an analyst is to say what a central bank will do, not what it should, but in this case we think the criteria the BoC has laid out for itself make the case for waiting for more clarity on the outlook before moving again. So while we would not be the least bit surprised if the BoC cut another 25bp next week, we continue to look for the BoC to stay on hold and then ease again in September.

The case for patience starts with Governor Macklem suggesting that the Bank’s easing cycle would be “gradual”, alongside an assessment that cutting too rapidly “could jeopardize the progress we’ve made” on inflation. While it’s possible that the BoC gets two 25bp cuts out of the way quickly before going on hold for a meeting or more, we think the backdrop of gradual easing by other central banks—particularly with the Fed likely but not certain to ease by September and then quarterly thereafter—reinforces the case for a hold next week. Against that, the BoC may not want to surprise markets that are nearly fully priced for a 25bp cut this month, although it may not wish to be beholden to them either.

Figure 1: Core CPI measures
![](images/c8ac62dfc793f1d7f0306f0a29b5d9df662466741df704914ddf4602c4718dac.jpg)

A second aspect of the case for easing would be the lack of clear further progress on inflation. Since the June rate cut the May CPI came in hot and the June print was not cool. Both the BoC’s preferred core measures as well as the exclusionary core have firmed in recent months, with 3-month and 12- month annualized core inflation rates at the top of the BoC’s target band (Figure 1). Meanwhile, the latest Business Opinion Survey showed a slimmer majority (51%) than in 1Q seeing inflation within that 1% to 3% band, in part because the non-response rate jumped—likely a sign of greater inflation uncertainty among firms. It thus would be consistent with pri or communications for the Bank to suggest that it would prefer to wait for additional signs of a sustained cooling in inflation toward 2% before easing further. Recall that the Summary of Deliberations for the June meeting indicated a willingness to wait to ease then given the risks to inflation.

A counter-argument may come from the real side of the economy. A disappointing May retail sales report points to sub-1% real GDP growth for 2Q, although the monthly GDP measures are suggesting closer to 2%. Yes, the most recent labor market report disappointed with jobs shedding 1.4K (although employment is still up 1% over the first six months of 2024), and the unemployment rate now at 6.4%—up 1.6%-pts from its cycle low. Yet even as business wage growth expectations came down in the 2Q BOS, actual wage growth surprised higher in last month’s report. The first of these was already know at the June policy meeting; the second is an unwelcome subsequent surprise. Putting it all together, the BoC may yet decide to cut next week, but the economic evidence makes a more compelling case to wait in our view.

## Retail sales slump in May

A disappointing May retail sales report points to a softer trajectory for real goods consumption in 2Q24. Retail sales fell 0.8% in May, with core retail sales—which exclude gasoline station and auto dealer sales—down 1.3% in May. Real retail sales declined 0.7% on the month (Figure 2). The June flash estimate was also downbeat, suggesting that total nominal retail sales fell a further 0.3% last month. In response, we have revised down our 2Q24 real consumption forecast from 3.0%ar to 2.5%ar. However, given we are still only about halfway though the quarter in terms of data and there remains significant uncertainty about the inventory contribution, we hold our 2Q24 real GDP growth forecast at 0.8%ar.

Figure 2: Real retail sales and goods PCE %3m/3m, saar (PCE is %q/q, saar)
![](images/519ca1b32b32d4d65edb05a6102b72aa1f2ef086d8b11e190e00125f15cb2d2e.jpg)

## Mexico

1H July inflation to confirm encouraging core dynamics despite short-term non-core concerns

• May GDP expected to deliver balanced gains but not enough to boost 2Q growth as originally expected

• May retail sales and June TB next week also to deliver encouraging news for consumption

• Policy continuation positive for consumption, less so for investment

Banxico dovishly pivoted in its latest meeting, and the shift was reinforced in the accompanying minutes two weeks later. The combination of slowing growth, progress made on the inflation front (particularly on core inflation) and the very tight current policy stance opened the room for easing in the next meeting. That said, there is still division within the board, and different data are being pondered differently.

Against this backdrop, the plot will likely thicken with next week’s inflation print. We expect headline inflation to move further north, reaching 5.25%oya, more than 1%-pt above the upper bound of the tolerance range, and a continuation of its months-long upward drift. The increase, again, would come on food price-pressures and, to a lesser extent, energy prices.

We are mindful that these increases can generate second round effects, but also that when focalized (think, for example of prices of items that account for 0.04% of the CPI basket rising north of 125%) these should be minimal. Furthermore, in the past, we’ve shown that headline inflation tends to converge to core and not the other way around. The current inflation setup could make for nonlinearities, though, calling for some caution. Be that as it may, we still think the underlying trend in prices is much less ambiguous in showing improvement.

We look for core inflation to slow to almost 4%, back within the target range, with momentum already running fairly below this rate. We expect 1H Jul core prices to rise 0.18%2w/2w (headline 0.36%), which yields a preliminary forecast for July of 0.27%m/m, sa, the fourth month of below-norm core price increases.

Unsurprisingly, this would pull down the 3m/3m annualized run rate to 3.4%, the lowest since the depths of the pandemic. Furthermore most services price-measures would continue to disinflate (Figure 1), with overall services price gains running at 4.3%3m/3m, saar and ex. housing and education at 4.2%, both multi-year lows. It is worth noting that the spike in headline year-ago inflation in 1H Jul is likely to be followed—all else equal—by a sharp downturn in 2H Jul on base effects

and signs in high frequency data of stabilization/decline in raw food prices. This would reinforce the view of looking at core rather than headline inflation for calibrating monetary policy, which we think is the dominant view within the board.

Figure 1: Core goods and services
![](images/efe8c867b9c41f58226167637664141bd1248e414ca9c2a2f57ad2f85a564c15.jpg)

## GDP: May/June lift would not salvage 2Q

Our hopes for a rebound in activity last quarter were largely dashed by the dismal April GDP print, specifically the confounding drop in services GDP. Data in May seem to offer partial respite in a handful of ways. Construction spending seems back on track after an early-year lull whereas consumer spending in both goods and services apparently bounces back. This should make for a solid May GDP print, which we expect to show a 0.6%m/m gain.

Figure 2: Credit card spending
![](images/4d2439b63aad27f3a0b94696098639db09233aaf676756adc87edcd2ea2b5442.jpg)

Credit card spending surged 3%m/m, sa (our adjustment) in May (Figure 2), ANTAD same-store sales surged 8.1%oya, and consumer fundamentals remain very solid. We expect this to translate into both strong retail sales (+1.1%m/m, sa) and services output (+0.7%, which includes retail sales as commercial services). That said, even incorporating another solid gain in June GDP, our 2Q GDP growth forecast of 3.5%ar would still look too optimistic, and should likely look closer to 1.5%-2%.

Looking a bit further ahead, there are a handful of reasons why we are not overly concerned about the disappointing news in 2Q. First, momentum should be stronger going into

2H even if new risks are on the rise, particularly fixed investment. Second, public spending should prove less of an overhang in 2H, as it was not as front-loaded as we thought ahead of the June election, notably infrastructure spending. While the correction is ongoing (Figure 3), the budget execution was below expectations, at 50%, by the end of 1H.

While signs are increasing that global manufacturing is lifting, mixed signals about its outlook in the context of geopolitical challenges and tensions is expected to hurt external demand for Mexican exports—and more so on the back of a still structurally strong currency. Yes, there is some upside for 3Q but overall, business cycle conditions are softening and consistent with subpar growth ahead.

Figure 3: IP and political cycles - Infrastructure Index, t = 100; Start of each presidential term
![](images/3d04d7d54c14f92422f50d31d44be2d88f4f47ca9bd701f9630e591ab1c5976b.jpg)

## Cross-currents in economic sentiment

The dichotomy between firm domestic consumption and softer external demand is behind the large disappointment in economic activity since last year, but with business sentiment now sliding further given institutional uncertainty after the June election—which could be aggravated by political uncertainty in the region in 2H—we believe domestic conditions will deliver mixed news and will potentially diverge from each other (i.e. strong consumption and soft gross fixed investment) in a similar fashion to 2018-19.

Consumer confidence gained further traction in June, right after the election, reaching its maximum since the 2018 election on the back of expectations for the continuation of economic policies founded on heavy social spending; however business sentiment soured (Figure 4) on the uncertainty related to the forthcoming legislative months in which a reform agenda aimed at further centralization of power is expected. Also, the 2025 budget to be announced in November is expected to hurt public capital spending in order to correct the arising fiscal pressures.

Of note, this week Fitch decided to give the Mexican government the benefit of the doubt and kept its credit rating outlook unchanged (Stable). Still the rating agency warned that institutional deterioration, under-par growth, and a fiscal miss could result in a less optimistic message next year. We expect at least one agency to change the outlook to negative next year. Both S&P and Moody’s have a higher rating than Fitch so the bar is lower for the former two.

## Brazil

Recent events raised questions about the sustainability the fiscal framework and commitment to its targets

• The government announced BRL15bn in freezes and blocks—not enough to achieve the target

• Healthy private balance sheets, partly at the expense of strained fiscal accounts, keep GDP growth robust

We revised up our 2024 GDP forecast to 2.5%y/y, just four-tenths below last year’s performance

In the middle of questions about the sustainability of the government’s fiscal framework and the level of commitments to its targets, all eyes were on the government spending freezes and expectations for this year’s fiscal result. Neither we nor the consensus expect the government to deliver the targeted balanced primary (ex. interest rates) result this year, coming from a 2.3% deficit last year, but high public expenditures growth so far in the year challenges the fiscal framework and raises the risk of an early end to the credibility of this tool that reduced the fiscal risks last year. In this context, solid signals of spending restraint tend to reduce the market stress.

Against this backdrop, the government announced a temporary cut of BRL15bn, at the upper range of our expectations, even if we recognize that something somewhat above that announcement is required given the fiscal performance in the first half of the year. In our view, delivering this BR15bn in freezes and presenting more details on the measures announced by Finance Minister Fernando Haddad to contain spending growth and raise additional revenues could help decompress risk. Next week’s fiscal review reports detailing revenues and expenditures for 2024 will be thoroughly scrutinized, as it may give us some signals about the government’s ability to keep the balanced target in the 2025 budget proposal, which must be delivered by August 31 (Table 1).

Table 1: Budget timeline

Presidential sanction</td></tr><tr><td>Jan</td><td>New fiscal year: approved budget or constrained expenditures kick in</td></tr></table>

Source: Congress, OECD and J.P. Morgan

The recent market stress has been related not only to debt-to-GDP considerations, but also to the need of further fiscal stimulus amid solid economic growth. One of our major views this year was that Brazil was well positioned to grow, on the back of healthy private-sector balance sheets that are partly sustained by strained fiscal balance sheets. In fact, this week we estimated that the large fiscal stimulus put in place since mid-2022 boosted last year’s growth by 0.5%-pt, inflation by almost 2%-pts up to 2Q and the SELIC rate by up to 3%-pts.

Even considering the meaningful negative impact the southern floods—a significant and concentrated shock—had on economic growth in May, economic activity remained resilient, suggesting the Brazilian economy has greater underlying strength than expected. With the rebound from coincident indicators in June, we revised up our 2Q GDP estimate to 2.5%, closer to the level suggested by our traditional economic activity models and the nowcaster (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Brazil 2Q nowcaster
![](images/7e877bf178a0d3a1c108bf83fae6a5a9fe5d12a3bb7036f7fe3d0dc44445e426.jpg)

Figure 2: GDP Index
![](images/7112815e284e40229b66365c579504593d5ed2f3892b8c0592e6c3d5b40d175e.jpg)

The resilience of the Brazilian economy continues beyond our above-consensus initial estimates. We then see 2024 GDP growing 2.5%y/y, a small step down from the 2.9% growth last year when the agricultural sector contributed to almost half of the expansion. In other words, while this positive supply shock is over, the strength of domestic demand is mostly compensating for it (Figure 2). We don't expect rate moves this year, but this strong growth amid exchange rate deprecia tion and rising inflation expectations—also partly a consequence of the fiscal issues—keep the market pricing policy rate hikes in the short run.

## Demand-related core CPI up slightly in Jun

Core CPI (ex. controlled and food CPI) decelerated a tenth to 3.1%oya in June, a tad above BCB’s 3% inflation target. By our calculations, the rise of core CPI from the bottom of 2.9% was due to a recovery of the contribution of demand-related core CPI, which is now at 1.2%-pts from below 1% a couple of months ago. This uptick was led mostly by the contribution of demand-related core goods CPI. The supply-related part moved back below 1%-pt in June (Figure 3, see here for full methodology).

## Argentina

• Entering phase II of policy framework

Headline balance posted 0.4% of GDP surplus YTD, the largest since 2008

• After the approval of the fiscal package, we revise up our 2024 primary surplus forecast to 1.2% of GDP

• We now watch for the upcoming decision on the PAIS tax and the new pension formula

Activity in May performed better than expected, increasing 1.3%m/m, sa

The authorities announced the transition into Phase II of the policy framework, which involves the monetary arrangement evolving into a more conventional one, which will lay the foundation for currency competition (defined as Phase III). In this second phase no innovation on the FX front is expected, as the central bank continues to construct the basis to remove the capital controls in the future. The message thus calls for the 2% crawling peg arrangement to be maintained, prioritizing the move of inflation expectations lower.

The so-called Phase II entails having the Treasury be responsible for the cost of liquidity absorption, solving the potential monetary issuance associated with the put options embedded in local Treasury bonds, and finally the recently announced shutdown of the money "faucet" associated with the purchase of USD. In this regard, during the weekend authorities announced the decision to sterilize the equivalent of ARS2.5tn via supplying hard currency in the parallel FX market. The authorities were emphatic in distinguishing that the aim is to continue with the reduction of the stock of pesos in the economy, targeting peso issuance and not an FX level (sterilization, not intervention). They acknowledged the temporary nature of this arrangement, as it only works under capital controls and the objective is to liberalize the capital account when conditions permit. Moreover, market conditions are to be assessed when determining whether or not to supply hard currency in the parallel market (i.e. BCRA’s own discretion in terms of timing of intervention). Against this backdrop, the recent policy announcements show that in terms of priorities, accelerating the disinflation dominates over reserve accumulation, creating doubts about the ability and velocity to elevate the external debt repayment capacity ahead. The path, though narrow, aims at first building credibility via disinflation, to later remove capital controls and regain market access. Credibility is linked to inflation expectations converging to the 2% monthly crawling peg rate and parallel FX to the importer FX level.

## A positive (cash-basis) fiscal result in June

Despite the adverse spending seasonality that characterizes the month of June, the Treasury reported a US\$0.5bn equivalent primary surplus on the month, the sixth in a row. The YTD primary surplus thus reached 1.2% of GDP, compared to the 1.6% of GDP shortfall reported in the same period of 2023. The headline balance (including interest payments) posted a 0.4% of GDP surplus YTD, the largest since 2008 and the first headline surplus in the month of June since 2010. This represents a sizable fiscal consolidation compared to the 2.7% of GDP shortfall in same period of 2023. On a last 12-month basis, the headline balance is running at a 0.7% of GDP deficit, compared to a 4.4% shortfall at year-end.

When breaking down by component, primary revenues saw payback from last month’s extraordinary income tax collection increase (see our recent note), declining by 17.3%m/m, sa from +19.1% the prior month. On an over-year-ago basis, primary revenues were down by 14.6%oya on the month (-4.8%oya YTD), as compared to a -3.0%oya average in the prior three months. When controlling for one-off increases in income and “PAIS” taxes, on a sequential basis, primary revenues were down by 4.6%m/m, sa on the month, with negative momentum accelerating to -38.8%3m/3m, saar.

On the spending side, the over-year-ago contraction in primary spending remains broad-based, with the spending contraction pace accelerating on the month to 35.1%oya in real terms, as compared to -26.9%oya in the prior three months (Figure 1). On a sequential basis, primary spending also declined by 7.2%m/m, sa, on the heels of the 7.4% plunge seen the prior month.

Figure 1: Primary revenues and expenditures %oya last 12 months, deflated by CPI
![](images/6b8e6b989eeddaffbfcf8106f7e5058a9ce754927780a9d47adbe0c21af3ed26.jpg)
Source: Ministry of Treasury and J.P. Morgan

As emphasized previously, the primary spending decline has been spearheaded by social transfers, explaining 39% of the 1H24 spending adjustment, followed by capital spending (22%), consumption and provincial transfers (17%) and economic subsidies (16%). In terms of levels, primary spending is 32.3% below the levels seen in July-November 2023, with social transfers down by 21.6%, economic subsidies down by 59.9% and capital spending by a remarkable 75%.

Primary deficit

Following the approval of the Omnibus bill and fiscal package in Congress, we revise higher our 2024 annual primary surplus forecast to 1.2% of GDP. Note that the changes approved as part of the fiscal package (income tax re-incorporation, tax amnesty and moratorium, and personal goods tax amendments) jointly accounted for 0.7% of GDP in additional fiscal revenues, prompting us to revise our primary forecast by an equivalent amount. This would be consistent with a headline deficit of 0.5% of GDP on the year.

We now watch for the upcoming decision related to the ‘Impuesto PAIS’ tax, as the Ministry of Finance anticipated they could reduce it from 17.5% to 7.5% following the Omnibus bill and fiscal package regulation. If starting in September, that reduction could result in 0.3%-pt of GDP in tax revenue sacrifice in our estimates.

On the other side, we also watch for the fate of the new pension formula, which was approved by the Lower House and is pending discussion in the Senate. While the fiscal cost of the amended formula could be sizeable in the medium term if real wages increase (see our recent note), the government is said to be negotiating amendments to the original bill to eventually limit the cost if the bill is approved.

## A May green shoot

Activity in May performed better than expected, with the monthly activity indicator gaining by 1.3%m/m when adjusting for seasonality and calendar effects, thus ending a sequence of nine monthly contractions. The 3-month sequential momentum remained in negative territory, at -3.2%ar, but absent revisions, it aligns well with our base case scenario for the second quarter to print slightly positive, at +0.5%ar.

Regarding ex. agriculture activity, our estimates also show a monthly gain, of 1.0%m/m, sa in April. In the same vein as headline activity, May posted a sequence of eight consecutive contractions, accumulating a drop of 1.8%sa.

As discussed in prior months, the decoupling of headline and ex. agricultural activity proved more marked in the second quarter of the year, on an over-year-ago basis. This is due because the recovery of agri conditions proved a boost for growth on base effects, largely masking the contraction of ex. primary production. Indeed, headline activity printed +2.3%oya in May, while ex.agriculture fell 5.0%oya. C

Compared to recent activity collapses, the recession at this stage is proving in between the levels observed after the 2018 sudden stop and the macro-correction induced early on in the Macri administration (Figure 2). That said, it’s worth noting that, as Figure 1 shows, the current economic activity level is below those observed in 2018 and similar to 2012’s lows.

Figure 2: Comparing the current recession to prior events Real activity, index, sa, (t=0) pre recession peak level =100
![](images/dcc4e3fd6db9054a863210f88860cd99ea9bbf2fa25704fe37521b74690f32a9.jpg)

Our base case is for activity to stabilize in the second quarter, with sequential GDP turning a bit into positive terrain (+0.5%ar) after the collapse in 1Q (-9.9%ar). We maintain the annual GDP forecast at -3.4%y/y.

The authors wish to thank Juan Goldin, of the Latin America Economics Research team, JPMorgan Chase Bank Sucursal Buenos Aires, for his contribution to this report.

## Andeans

• Colombia: Monthly activity tracker contracted by 0.4%m/m, sa

When adjusting for seasonal and calendar effects, May activity gained by 2.5%oya (+2.0% YTD)

• 2Q24 GDP is forecasted at 2.0%ar, though risks are tilted to the upside

• Peru: Monthly headline activity gained slightly in May, +0.1%m/m

The annual GDP projection is maintained at +3.0%y/ y; risks continue to be tilted to the upside

## Colombia: May’s mild activity payback

The monthly activity index reported by DANE indicates a slight decline in economic activity following a strong performance in April (see note). When adjusting for seasonality and calendar effects, headline activity contracted by 0.4%m/m, sa, thus only partially reversing the leap higher observed in April (+2.0%m/m, sa). The revised sequential growth path converged to 2.6% 3m/3m saar, a tad below potential, which compares to a revised 5.6%ar in 1Q24. Through May, economic activity increased by 2.0% when compared to December ’23. On an annual basis and correcting for calendar effects, activity printed +2.5%oya (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Colombia economic activity tracker
![](images/7f810e98e7546e180fa9683346a77ed50abda24e1237d07be1a646ba6c8ed331.jpg)

The supply-side breakdown reveals that the monthly contraction was primarily driven by a decline in primary production, which fell by 0.25%m/m, sa, while secondary and tertiary production printed flat in the month. Despite the May pothole, it’s worth highlighting that primary production has risen by +4.9%, sa, YTD. On its side, tertiary production performance has also proved positive, up by 2.5%, sa YTD. Of note, the breakdown of services shows that the good performance has been driven by gains in public administration, up by 11.7% YTD, which more than offset the plunge in financial and insurance services (-6.7% YTD). On the other side, secondary production (manufacturing and construction) has evidenced subpar momentum compared to the other sectors, with a modest gain of 0.3% YTD.

Overall, despite the monthly decline in May, headline activity has performed better than initially expected at the beginning of the year, expanding by 2.0%, sa YTD. In terms of forecasts, while acknowledging the usual volatility of the economic activity tracker, we raise our 2Q24 forecast to 3.4%ar (2.2%oya), though high frequency data tilt risks to the upside. The annual GDP growth projection is revised a tad higher, to +2.0%y/y (consistent with +2.7%4Q/4Q).

## Peru: Robust 2Q24 activity momentum

Monthly headline activity gained slightly in May, following a significant increase in April. When adjusting for seasonality and calendar effects, the activity index logged 0.1 %m/m, sa, coming on the heels of +1.7%m/m, sa in April. The sequential momentum thus converged to 8.7 %3m/3m saar, and leaves a carryover for 2Q of 9.6%ar. When measured on an over-year-ago basis, base effects continue to underpin the sequential performance, for headline activity to record 5.0%oya (Figure 2). When adjusting for calendar effects, growth eases to +4.3%oya.

Figure 2: Peru real economic activity
![](images/3f230fb69dbcb5dbac4336989f5e1b80d0eee16c8590adb30dd12f5a6df233da.jpg)

When breaking down activity on the supply side, mining stood out, contributing 0.5%-pt to the headline monthly change. Conversely, manufacturing (-0.2%-pt) and agriculture (-0.3%-pt) showed a disappointing performance. By aggregation, the lion’s share of May’s headline gain was explained by secondary sectors, with a positive 0.4%-pt contribution on the month.

In all, activity continues to run its upward recovery trend this year, leaving behind the mediocre performance observed post-COVID recovery. Indeed, by May headline activity printed 5.7 % above pre-pandemic levels as of February 2020.

The activity data so far align with our expectation for the secondary sector to drive growth in the second quarter, with services playing a more significant role in the second half of the year. Disinflation should continue to help households regain real purchasing power, while easier monetary conditions and improved terms of trade should support gains in non-residential capital expenditures (capex). On the other hand, we expect public sector investment to decelerate in the coming two quarters, while net real exports are likely to contribute negatively to GDP.

Regarding forecasts, assuming no revisions to the series, 2Q24 GDP is revised to+9.0%q/q. If confirmed, sequential momentum should ease in 3Q to levels as of 1.7%ar, to accelerate again in 4Q to +4.2%ar. The annual GDP projection is maintained at +3.0%y/y, consistent with 4.2% 4Q/4Q. Forecast risk continues to be tilted to the upside.

The authors wish to thank Juan Goldin, of the Latin America Economics Research team, JPMorgan Chase Bank Sucursal Buenos Aires, for his contribution to this report.

## United Kingdom

• Another upside but narrow surprise on services inflation

Trend in wage growth remains firm, but latest data soften

• August MPC call looks close, but we continue to look for a cut

Both wage and services inflation continue to run at a strong pace. Taken with a clear growth upswing in 1H24, this does weaken the argument for lowering rates. Nevertheless, the minutes of the June MPC meeting appeared to guide towards an August cut. And while GDP has turned out stronger than the Committee expected since then, the labour market continues to loosen and incremental surprises in the wage and price data this week are hard to describe as significant. The surprise on services inflation in June was narrowly confined to one sector, and the MPC previously looked through a much bigger surprise in the April data. The incremental news on pay this week was also touch weaker. While Huw Pill’s hawkish remarks from last week even up the odds of a cut in August, we think the BoE will move on a narrow vote.

## A narrow inflation surprise

UK headline CPI inflation for June delivered a small upside surprise, remaining at 2.0% (JPMorgan 1.9; Consensus 1.9; BoE 2.0). Likewise, core also held stable at 3.5% (JPMorgan 3.4; Consensus 3.4). Against expectations for a slowing, services inflation remained steady at 5.7% (JPMorgan 5.4; Consensus 5.6; BoE 5.1). RPI was down from 3.0% to 2.9% (JPMorgan 2.9; Consensus 2.9).

Based on our seasonal adjustment, we estimate that headline CPI prices rose 0.2%m/m, sa in June with core prices up 0.3%. Core goods surprised a little to the downside with a drop of 0.2%, helped by a decline in clothing prices. But services again came in firm with a 0.5% gain. We had expected a softer print of closer to 0.2% due to some payback from the outsized gain in airfares seen in the prior month. Not only did that not happen, but it came alongside a large 1.7% gain in the category that includes package holidays and accommodation. Accommodation prices rose 3.2%m/m, sa on our estimate, which with a weight of 3% added around 0.1% to the CPI (and twice this to monthly services inflation). See Table 1.

Accommodation is where we would expect to see a potential Taylor Swift impact. We were skeptical of this kind of impact because the UK price collection date was well away from the London tour dates, as well as being in between rather than during the the Edinburgh and Liverpool dates (see table

below). In Sweden, the concerts were concentrated in Stockholm and the price collection dates used by the SCB overlapped with the tour. In any case, the Swedish impact helped to boost non-housing services by 1.5%m/m, which suggests a hospitality impact on services that is around six times larger than seen in the UK in June (regardless of the driver).

Other parts of services were much better behaved in June outside of rent and housing. The MPC has often mentioned a measure of persistence weighted services inflation that excludes both airfares and accommodation. As these were the main surprises, we estimate the MPC’s persistence measure was soft at 0.3%m/m, sa in June, with the year ago rate falling from 5.8% to 5.6%.

Some other derivatives of services inflation were firm as they include one or both of these categories. The MPC has tended to use different exclusion based measures at different points in time over the past nine months, so its no longer clear which they are most focussed on. But for those members with a bias to cut, its seems unlikely that a narrow and potentially temporary accommodation-led price rise should make a difference. Recall in the June minutes that the MPC downplayed the huge broad-based surprise in April inflation. Given that, it is hard to argue that June should materially alter the picture.

## A close call for August, nevertheless

One important caveat that we have often made in the past is the implicit bias associated with using exclusion based measures, especially when the items being excluded are rotated on a frequent basis as the MPC has done over recent months. When there is a big surprise, it is never totally broad-based, and can often easily be dismissed as narrow or a “one off” special factor. But when there is a series of “one-offs” moving from one component to another over a period of time, the risk it that systemic pressures are missed or disregarded.

The MPC needs to be mindful of this, with services inflation still up at 5.7%oya and the 3m/3m, saar at 7.7%. It is also the case that services inflation has come in at 5.8% for 2Q as a whole, which is well above the BoE’s 5.3% forecast and not related to a single print. For a central bank that has aligned its guidance to focus on services inflation, this has to matter with the surprises still coming. We have also revised up our inflation forecast owing to an expected 10% gain in Ofgem regulated energy bills in October. Our new forecast shows headline averaging 2.7% in 4Q (previously 2.4) with core at 3.4% (previously 3.3%).

Another important question is whether the MPC has the numbers to cut. Huw Pill recently appeared to indicate - i.e. even before this June CPI release - that he was not minded to vote for a cut in August. We have sympathy with the way he is

reading the data. In contrast, Broadbent appeared ready to cut but has left the committee, and the views of his replacement (Lombardelli) are not known. Given that an August cut would therefore probably end up being something like a 5-4 vote, its hard to have a lot of confidence in the outcome. This is a big uncertainty, especially in light of the point above.

There are continued signs of moderation in some of the pricing surveys the MPC has chosen to focus on recently (e.g. DMP, PMI). Some on the MPC appear sensitive to the optics of inflation back at 2% briefly and there is likely also some implicit pressure as most other central banks have already cut or are expected to do so soon. That said, growth looks to be doing better than the MPC expected and should reduce urgency.

## Few surprises in labour market report

The latest UK labour market report was close to expectations, with the ILO unemployment rate holding at 4.4% and employment from the LFS household survey showing a weak, but positive, gain of 19k through to May (or 0.2%, ar, 3m/3m). This marks an end to the period of falling employment on this (quite volatile) measure that has been present since January.

The separate and more timely PAYE payroll-based jobs measure rose 16k in June, with an upward revision to the prior month from -3k to +54k. This measure is showing growth of 0.5% on a 3m/3m, ar basis. Vacancies continued to fall, but with the vacancy rate stable at 2.8%. This is just a shade above the pre-pandemic level, but the V/U ratio is slightly below that level, due to the recorded rise in the unemployment rate (which still has measurement problems due to a low sample rate, see below\*).

The average weekly earnings data show a slowing in non-bonus pay from 6.0% to 5.7%oya (3mma) in May, which equates to a 0.4% gain over the month. This was as expected. The private sector figure came in a touch above our forecast, slowing from 5.8% to 5.5%, but this was a small surprise due to rounding. The surprise was also driven by an upward revision to the prior month, when the increase in the National Living Wage (NLW) occurred. In May, private pay was actually softer at 0.3%m/m. That marks a clear slowdown compared to the prior three months, when monthly gains had averaged a 0.7% pace.

We also note that the timelier PAYE payroll-based wage data showed a 0.4%m/m drop in the median for June, and a 0.5%m/m decline in the private sector proxy we have constructed (Figure 1). This is the weakest monthly outturn seen during the post-pandemic period, although these data are less reliable and include bonuses. In contrast, the Indeed wage tracker remained elevated and stable at 6.8% in June.

The run rate on the AWE private pay data themselves also remains strong, with the 3m/3m, annualized rate at 7.3%, even if partly related to the NLW rise. Earnings on this measure also still appear on track to slightly exceed the BoE’s 2Q forecast of 5.1%oya, by a tenth. There is little in the release that looks decisive regarding the MPC. The call on the BoE still looks close. But we stick with August on the basis of the signalling from the June minutes (which downplayed prior strength in the data) and the absence of material data surprises on wages and services inflation since then. The MPC can still cut without Pill’s vote.

Figure 1: AWE vs. PAYE private sector pay measures %m/m, inc.. bonuses, PAYE is median
![](images/00fff868fa270b19b580e057554faf78c84026717a0b81a12969892647b4151a.jpg)
\* The ONS issued a notice in the press release stating that it no longer plans to shift to the new online TLFS survey this September. After having indicated that the early signs were positive, with this measure being the long-term solution, it has announced there is a significant response bias in the survey (e.g. towards older participants). The online survey also

Data releases and forecasts Week of July 22-26

suffers from not having an interviewer to ask for clarification when answers are unclear, as well as incomplete survey responses. The ONS will continue to develop the new TLFS until these problems are resolved, but doesn’t intend to report back on this until 1Q25. The ONS has said, however, that TLFS sample and response rates are higher than the current LFS and that it remains confident in the concept as a solution. In the meantime, the plan is to continue to boost the response rate of the existing LFS survey, which will remain the lead measure.

## Retail sales fall, consumer confidence up

Retail sales fell 1.2%m/m in June, partially unwinding the large 2.9% spike in the prior month. Sales have been very choppy during the second quarter, in part due to weather patterns. Overall sales were down 0.7% on a q/q annualized basis in 2Q, although the large 1Q gain has been broadly sustained. Mechanically the June report subtracts 0.1% from GDP in that month. Even allowing for this, there is upside risk to our 2Q growth forecast of 2% annualized.

Separately, Gfk consumer confidence (Figure 2) edged up again from -14 to -13 in July, the highest now since September 2021 and back close to its average. Major purchases have been depressed for a while, but took a big leap forward from -23 to -16, another sign that consumers are finally willing to spend their income gains. The PMI has sent a negative momentum signal about growth of late. We look for a small gain next week, but in any case the fundamentals for the consumer still look positive.

## Emerging Europe

• Israel: Core inflation momentum elevated

• Romania: Another soft core in June

• Russia: CBR expected to hike 100bp next week

## Israel: Core inflation momentum elevated

Israel’s inflation accelerated to 2.9%oya in June from 2.8%, in line with consensus, but a tad above our forecast. Relative to our expectations, food and services surprised higher. Sequential momentum in headline CPI firmed to an estimated 3.1%m/m, saar, from 2.2% the previous month, bucking the global trend for softer dynamics in June. Momentum in core inflation firmed to around 4%m/m, saar, on our estimates (from negative the previous month pulled down by airfares).

The main pressure points remained the same and are mostly related to supply constraints amid recovering domestic demand. Services are most problematic, with rent inflation and house price gains staying elevated amid reduced construction activity. Owner equivalent rent rose 8.0%m/m, saar, last month (estimates are based on new and renewed lease contracts only); actual rents added 2.9%. Outside of housing, services appear fairly well-behaved, with tourism (airfares) adding to volatility in recent months. Momentum in core goods firmed to about 1.5% saar in the past couple of months, from \~0.7% in the prior four months amid increased obstacles to maritime trade, but this does not appear too different from global trends. Excluding volatile fruit and vegetables, momentum in food continued moderating and we think the trend will continue.

Smoothing through recent volatility in the main inflation indices, it seems that the underlying inflation dynamics remain too firm for the BoI to consider policy easing in the near term, especially as geopolitical uncertainty remains high. Yet, we expect the housing impulse to ease somewhat going into autumn, which should alleviate part of inflation concerns – we expect inflation to end this year at around 3.0%oya and next year at \~2.7% (assuming 1%-pt VAT hike). We continue to anticipate the next BoI cut coming in November, contingent on decline in Israel’s geopolitical risk premia.

Figure 1: Israel core goods and services momentum
![](images/f1ecb352f0f8ed56a511e57ead289eae4bd63968fad913223a38ad3e94c3910a.jpg)
Source: Haver, J.P. Morgan

## Romania: another soft core in June

Last week’s Romania headline CPI for June slowed a bit less than expected: 4.9%oya from 5.1% (we had 4.8%), but core was softer than in our forecast. NBR expected 6% for headline in June and the large downside surprise over April-May for the central bank is now confirmed. There is disinflation in all main components (Figure 2), but non-food disinflation is heavily impacted by changes in regulated energy prices, while services inflation remains most elevated. An aspect worth highlighting is that core inflation was, for the second month in a row, softer than expected: 5.7%oya from 6.3% and 5.8% expected (NBR also expected 5.8%, same as our forecast). The upside surprise in headline against soft core and volatile food (-3.2%oya) was driven by a large hike in the price of natural gas (9.2%m/m) which added 0.3%-pts to headline.

Disinflation in core is driven by both processed food and market-based goods and services (Figure 3). Decline in inflation for the latter has accelerated over May-June, but food inflation also shifted lower, despite being already at low rates. There isn’t much space left in processed food disinflation and most of the forecasted decline is coming from market-based goods and services.

Momentum for the main components of core inflation suggests two important aspects: 1. momentum has downshifted significantly, except for goods and 2. further disinflation in over-year-ahead core is ahead of us. For overall core, the 3m saar momentum downshifted to about 5% from 6% in May. Processed food, which slowed to 0.5% 3m saar from 1.5% in May, continued to help core disinflation but both core goods and services also softened, each more than expected. However, 3m saar core goods momentum remains rather problematic at 8% saar from 8.5% in May. There was a large drop in June, but it remains to be seen if it persists – we tend to think it will. Core services are at a lower level, but 6% saar remains significantly above the 2.5% target. We continue to take comfort from the slowing in core services (in line with labor market softening) as we see no strong macro rationale for core goods to stay at high levels. We expect further slowing in core inflation to drag the over-year-ago measure lower too.

The NBR might react to June inflation even though it has already cut the policy rate on downside inflation surprises that materialized in April-May. As those two months widened a large gap versus the NBR forecast, if the new forecast – to be approved at the August meeting – shows significant downside revisions, the NBR might cut again. We have the next 25bp rate cut in November, but see good chances for the NBR to move again in August – ongoing softness in core helps, even though it is mild relative to our NBR forecast.

## Russia: CBR preview

We expect the CBR to make its move next week and hike 100bp to 17.0%. The CBR has been quite open about its hawkish leanings, and market consensus seems to be gravitating toward a larger 200bp hike. Our sense is that the macro picture does not warrant harsh action by the CBR (yet), while normal market functioning suggests that an emergency hike should not be on the agenda. The economy remains as overheated as a few months ago and the CBR’s disappointment about the lack of policy transmission is understandable. But given the potential for longer-than-usual transmission lags in this unusual environment, as well as recent tightening of macro-prudential regulation and government-sponsored credit programs, which should result in lower credit creation irrespective of level of policy rate, we think that giving some more time for the complex of policy measures to work through the system would be wise. Inflation momentum increased in May-July, partially on one-offs, but there is no

sense that inflation is running out of control. Besides, momentum in core inflation in June was less bad than feared. Therefore, although the CBR’s signalling leaves little chance for the key rate to be left unchanged, we sense that policy gradualism argument should prevail and a 100bp, not 200bp, hike will be delivered next week, even as the guidance will likely leave the door open for further potential tightening in case the data flow warrants.

## South Africa

• MPC kept the repo rate unchanged at 8.25% with mixed guidance and a 4/2 vote split

Inflation forecasts were lowered with risks viewed to the upside

We add a 25bp cut for September with the repo rate at 7.25% by mid-25

• Inflation likely cooled to 5.1%oya in June

The MPC kept the repo rate unchanged at 8.25%, as anticipated, but we think the SARB may be ready to cut at its next meeting (Figure 1). The SARB’s forecast revisions reflected a near-term improvement in inflation dynamics, in line with expectations. The inflation profile was lowered by 0.4%pts in 2H24 and 1H25, yet with the outlook from mid-25 onwards unchanged. This lowered the average inflation to 4.9% in 2024 (from 5.1%; JPMe: 4.9%) and 4.4% in 2025 (from 4.5%). For the next three quarters, headline inflation therefore should fall marginally below the targeted 4.5% (bottoming at 4.2% in 1Q25), but revert to the target mid-band by 3Q25. The revisions are a result of lower petrol price inflation, but a stronger rand input assumption (USD/ZAR 18.35) is also expected to help contain food and core inflation.

Figure 1: South Africa inflation outlook
![](images/31bf8239f37ae6fb1e84b27d33c89748ca35749a55ffd44b486bcf3153a361dd.jpg)

At the same time, recent weak economic performance lowered the output gap to -0.3% for this year (from -0.1%). The output gap is expected to fully close next year. Reduced loadshedding is expected to marginally lift actual and potential growth over the medium term. After an estimated 1.5%pt drag on GDP from power cuts last year, this is expected to fade to 0.2% this year (previously 0.5%) and 0.1% next year. Potential growth is now seen as 1.3% next year and 1.7% in 2026.

With inflation expected to mildly undershoot its target and a marginally negative output gap, the SARB will likely begin its easing cycle. Without a doubt, the repo rate currently is restrictive. The SARB uses several methods to calculate this; averaging the methods (as we understand them) would see the real rate currently at 3.6% against a neutral real rate of 2.6%. This suggests the policy currently is restrictive by about 100bps, even as inflation is about to marginally undershoot its target. The risks to these projections primarily tie to the rand and remaining concerns around local services inflation. In our view, a reasonably steady currency (e.g. with USD/ZAR up to 18.50, perhaps even 18.75 on average for the next two months), no material service inflation upside surprise locally and continued expected for a would sufficiently lower SARB’s concerns on inflation risks to proceed with a September repo rate cut. We increase the probability of such a scenario to about two-thirds for September (from 30% previously).

## Inflation likely eased to 5.1%oya in June

Lower transportation and food costs likely helped contain headline inflation at 5.1%oya in June (from 5.2% in May), following a 124c/l cut in petrol prices at the pump. Food inflation probably also recorded a gentle downshift to 4.2%oya (from 4.3%), but now is near its trough. We expect core inflation to have eased a tenth to 4.5%oya, even as services inflation probably inches higher with the quarterly rental inflation survey the key upside risk to the report.

## Australia and New Zealand

• Australia’s unemployment rate increased 0.1%-pt to 4.1% sa in the June report

• NZ CPI rose 0.4%q/q nsa, in line with our forecast but below consensus

• We retain our call for the RBNZ to cut 25bp in the August meeting

Australia’s unemployment rate increased one tenth to 4.1% in June (4.051% to three decimal places), in line with J.P. Morgan and consensus forecasts. Employment growth surprised to the upside (actual: +50K, J.P. Morgan/consensus: +20K forecast), but this was insufficient to fully offset the uptick in labor supply with the participation rate climbing to 66.9%. The jobless rate has now increased 0.5%-pt over the past year and, in our view, will continue to creep higher as lagged effects of interest rates weigh on labor demand.

With respect to monetary policy, this week’s number is broadly aligned with the RBA’s 2Q forecast and, in our view, doesn’t materially tip the scales one way or another for the upcoming August policy meeting. Instead, the focus remains firmly on the 2Q inflation print which based on the data in hand is tracking around 1%q/q, an outcome consistent with the RBA’s standing forecast. In our view, the current combination of unemployment and inflation (if realized) will be enough to see the RBA stay on hold this year with rate cuts likely in 1H25, though we appreciate August remains possible conditional on the incoming price data. Markets are now pricing around a 1/3 chance of a rate hike in August, probabilities which seem about fair given the pin risk on the CPI report.

## NZ CPI consolidates easing case

NZ's 2Q CPI came in softer than RBNZ/consensus expectations (+0.5%/0.6% respectively) and in line with our forecast at +0.4%q/q nsa. The details were largely as expected, with the goods categories which tend to dominate the higher frequency tracking data sluggish at +0.2%q/q, and services a bit stronger (+0.6%q/q), but still annualizing in the band. Headline %oya inflation moderated from 4%oya to 3.3%oya, and will drop in the 1-3% target band in 2H as base effects from last year's lapsing of COVID-period subsidies fall out.

Core metrics were benign again, with the benchmark 20% trimmed mean measure steady at +0.7%q/q nsa (Figure 1). This has been annualizing in the target band for 3 quarters (4 if we take out the impact of subsidies mentioned above). The finer trim, e.g. only taking out bottom and top 5%, was softer still, annualized at the bottom of the band. In 1Q, the distribution of right tail outcomes (proportion of items growing

above 2%q/q ar, 3% and 4%) had already normalized to prepandemic levels, and that persisted in 2Q.

Figure 1: New Zealand trimmed mean inflation
![](images/7046bc0a85c468ad0540d4541b33967856a51bca09ff4b0bca31e4a7c9fbb980.jpg)

For around six months we have expected a first RBNZ cut in August. We hold that view and think we will see a bit more priced into the August meeting (currently around 50/50 for a 25bp cut) if the labor market softens further as expected in the 2Q survey.
