## Greater China

• China: 2Q GDP misses expectations as activity remains fragile, unstable and uneven

• Continued housing price correction

• Downgrade full-year growth forecast

• First-take on 3<sup>rd</sup> Plenum communique

• Next week: China LPRs, Hong Kong CPI, trade, Taiwan export orders and IP

The latest GDP report and June activity data show that economic activity in China remains fragile, unstable and uneven. 2Q GDP came in below expectations, growing 4.7%oya. Our seasonal adjustment suggests the economy’s growth slowed significantly to 1.0% q/q saar in 2Q, following solid 1Q growth at 7.4%. In a breakdown by expenditure of the 5.0%oya real GDP growth in 1H, consumption contributed 3.0%-pt, gross capital formation 1.3%-pt, and net exports 0.7%-pt. In nominal terms, 2Q GDP rose 4.0%oya, compared to 4.2% in 1Q. The implied GDP deflator fell 0.7%oya in 2Q (vs. -1.1% in 1Q). The 4.1%oya nominal GDP growth in 1H24 marked the second worst on record (better than only 1H20 during COVID outbreak).

The contrasting performance between out-performing and under-performing sectors has not changed. The lift from manufacturing investment (rising 9.5%oya in 1H24), green sectors and exports (export volume rose 12.7%oya in Jan-May) is critical for China to maintain 5%oya growth in 1H. June IP came in above expectations, growing 5.3%oya, or 1.1% m/m sa by our estimate, following a moderate increase of 0.4% m/ m sa in May. However, the drag from the housing market (real estate investment declined 10.1%oya in 1H24) and unstable performance in domestic consumption hurt the recovery. Retail sales grew at a subdued 2.0%oya in June, falling 1.3% m/m sa (Figure 1).

Figure 1: China retail sales
![](images/6c7d3eabc5d465bf3d36b6e50a7bffdb780c2e84225dc57838f57c61911263fd.jpg)

The government has rolled out policy measures to address the weak links in the economy, but the effects seem limited. On the consumption side, the trade-in subsidy for NEV sales has been supportive for auto sales, but the trade-in subsidy for home appliances is still absent hence sales of household electronic appliances are weak (falling 7.6%oya in June). Overall, policy support is still biased on production (especially advanced manufacturing and green sectors).

## Continued housing price correction

The NBS 70-city home prices remained elevated in June. New home prices were down 4.9%oya or 0.7%m/m nsa, and secondary home prices were down 7.9%oya or 0.9%m/m nsa. June’s results follow May’s 4.3%oya or 0.7%m/m fall in new home prices, and 7.5%oya or 1.0%m/m fall in secondary home prices. Across city tiers, 70-city secondary home prices dropped 0.4%m/m nsa in Tier-1 cities, narrowing from -1.2% in May, with the first sequential gain at 0.2% for Beijing and for 0.5% Shanghai this year. Meanwhile, secondary home prices declined 0.9%m/m in both Tier-2 and Tier-3 cities. As of June, the 70 major cities’ new home prices declined 7.1% compared to the peak in August 2021, along with a 13.2% drop in secondary home prices (Figure 2).

Figure 2: China 70-city housing prices
![](images/63165111e5c53c55ad6fad2c16f59e8a4bac8dd876cfc9fb05f95d8aa9d308fd.jpg)

Performance continues to diverge between new home and secondary home sales, and between presales and sales of finished new homes, with lingering concerns about home delivery especially among private developers. The top 100 developers’ aggregate sales decline narrowed to 14% from May’s 32%oya, with a 4% rise for SOE developers, and declines of 41% for non-distressed POEs and 57% for distressed developers. This indicates the liquidity stress for private developers remains an issue despite existing policy efforts (e.g. white-list developers and bank support for normal working capital loans to developers, etc.). Major cities’ new home sales surged into the end of 1H, partly on quarter-end or mid-year seasonality, but the momentum seems to have largely faded entering July.

## Downgrade full-year growth forecast

In response to disappointing 2Q GDP data, we revise down our full-year growth forecast to 4.7%oya (previously: 5.2%).

Consumption will contribute 2.8%-pts, investment 1.5%-pts and net exports 0.4%-pt to GDP growth. We maintain our forecast that China’s economic growth will return to trend averaging 4.5%q/q saar in 2H, but reaching this pace will be somewhat delayed in that now we forecast 3Q GDP growth at 4.0%q/q saar (previously: 4.5%) and 4Q GDP growth at 5.0%q/q saar (previously: 4.5%) (Figure 3). The GDP deflator will remain negative at -0.5%oya in 2024, which implies nominal GDP growth of 4.1%oya (previously: 4.7%), the second lowest on record (the lowest was 2.7% in 2020).

Figure 3: China real GDP growth
![](images/bce9aa9e29f405ad3e1702dbc7506bdb7996d02a7c7fb62b746397eac47aa450.jpg)

Our forecast assumes that economic policy will turn neutral or modestly supportive in 2H, but that there is no additional stimulus. On the fiscal side, the emphasis will be on fully utilizing the fiscal space as approved in the March NPC meeting, i.e. to accelerate the issuance of special local government bonds in the coming months. On the monetary policy front, we expect a 10bp rate cut and a 25bp RRR cut later in 3Q, with TSF growth staying soft at 8.1%oya at year-end. The official full-year GDP growth target of “around 5%” risks a modest undershooting.

## First take on 3rd Plenum communique

The CPC (Communist Party of China) held the 3rd Plenum of the 20th Party Congress on July 15-18. The Plenum concluded on July 18 and approved the Decision on Further Compre hensively Deepening Reform and Promoting Chinese-style Modernization (“the Decision” hereafter). The communique delivered no major surprises (positive or negative), as expected. “Chinese-style modernization” refers to the goal to improve the socialist system with Chinese characteristics and promote the modernization of the nation’s governance system, under the Party leadership. By 2035, China aims to to establish a high-level socialist market economy, laying a solid foundation for building a socialist modern power by the end of the century. The communique also announced an interim target to complete reforms as laid out in the Decision by 2029, the 80<sup>th</sup> Anniversary of the People’s Republic of China.

The reform agenda laid out in the Decision maintains the allaround, multiple-objective policy framework. It is a re-packaging of the new development framework as emphasized since the 19th Party Congress in 2017, and most reform topics have appeared in various Party and government documents in recent years. It does not shift the policy priority to economic growth, nor does it shift the focus to security or self-sufficiency. The strategic plan is balanced, as stated in the communique, is guided by economic reform, and takes promoting social fairness and justice and improving people’s well-being as its starting and end point. We have emphasized the importance of several reform areas, such as fiscal reform, rural land reform and balanced strategy to promote manufacturing and service upgrade; all of these are only briefly mentioned in the Communique.

The Communique also reiterated that this year’s economic and social development goal has to be achieved. In the following week, we shall wait for the release of full-version of the Decision, and also closely watch the Politburo meeting to be held in late July that will lay out policy guidance in 2H (exact date not announced). We expect the macro policy will turn neutral or modestly accommodative, but the likelihood of additional stimulus is relatively low. (1) On the fiscal side, we expect the Politburo will call for full utilization of this year’s fiscal space. As the pace of issuance for special local government bonds has been lagging behind normal years, around 2 trillion yuan special local government bonds can be issued in 3Q. (2) On the monetary policy front, the latest development in domestic economic activity and external environment (especially the changing expectations that Fed will likely announce first rate cut in September) strengthen our forecast of PBOC’s rate cut (likely in September) and RRR cut. (3) It is also interesting to see whether the government will step up efforts to stabilize the housing market and to support consumption, two critical issues to address the economic imbalances faced by the Chinese economy at present.

## Hong Kong: U rate unchanged at 3%

Hong Kong’s unemployment rate stayed at 3.0% sa for the three months ending June. Total employment rose 6.4k to 3,703.3k, while the labor force rose 4.9k to 3,818.0k. Seasonally adjusted, both total employment and labor force stayed unchanged in m/m sa terms. Employment across sectors showed diverse movements with modest gains in public administration, social personal services, finance insurance, real estate and professional services, and loss in retail, accommodation and food services.

Business indicators deteriorated in June. The SME business receipt current diffusion index fell 1.6pt to 41.4. Total economy PMI fell 1.1pt to 48.2, and the employment component fell 0.6pt to 49.0. The imbalance in cross-border travel activities continues to weigh on consumption and tourism-related sectors. The city’s unemployment rate has returned to the lowest level since the onset of the pandemic, and we expect it to remain around 3% in the near term.

## Korea

• KRW depreciation pushed up imported prices in June

• Exports volume index rose in June with tech sector’s growth

• Next week: GDP to take a technical correction

With no major data released this week, we wait for next week’s 2Q real GDP growth flash report. After the strong 5.3%q/q, saar growth in 1Q, we expect a technical payback with 2Q growth slowing to 0.5%q/q, saar. While this week’s June exports volume index suggest a smart rise in 2Q real exports growth, imports volume index also rose robustly in 2Q to suggest only a modestly positive contribution from net exports in 2Q. In the domestic demand detail, construction investment growth should be a drag according to the monthly data tracking, offsetting the positive contribution from net exports and consumption. In the production side detail, we expect a modest gain of the monthly GDP proxy (non-farm all-industry output) in June, consistent with barely positive quarterly real GDP growth (Figure 1).

Figure 1: Real GDP and non-farm all-industry output
![](images/205744503c8e7f912220f2a07c30b47ef086b55a7dd77b91c853c62e7273cc9d.jpg)

## Imports prices rose in June

If seasonally adjusted by J.P. Morgan, the imports price index rose 1.1%m/m, sa in local currency terms in June, and the three-month trend growth accelerated 7.6%3m/3m, saar in June after a moderation from March to May.

Figure 2: Imports price by currency terms
![](images/1df24823b01b1cb540748496a4a690067768c6928f83fc34fce9bd1e85694310.jpg)
Source: BoK, and J.P. Morgan

The currency depreciation was a major contributor to the imported price gain in June and 2Q, as in contractual currency terms the imports price index fell 0.3%m/m, sa in June and the three-month trend rose a relatively modest 1.7%3m/3m, saar (Figure 2). By product category, imported food prices rose a somewhat stronger 3.3%m/m, sa, while energy imports prices fell 0.3% and core imported prices (ex. food and energy) rose 2.4%.

Figure 3: Terms of trade
![](images/55f13da74ea01168c95d76e03a0ec42e823a478b64266c14cacb768def5da98c.jpg)
Source: BoK and J.P. Morgan. \* The price indices are on a contract-date basis.

Meanwhile, exports prices rose 2.2%m/m, sa in local currency terms in June while exports price in contractual currency terms rose a modest 0.5%. IT product exports prices rose 1.7% to lead the overall exports price gain, and the terms of trade index recovered in June, reflecting softer imports prices amid a gradual recovery in tech product exports prices (Figure 3).

## Exports volume index rose in June

Along with the terms of trade’s gradual and slow recovery, exports volume index also rose in June by 1.5%m/m, sa if seasonally adjusted by J.P. Morgan. In the three-month trend, exports volume index recovered to 11.4%3m/3m, saar from a 2.3% contraction in March. Tech exports’ volume growth was particularly strong, up 6.1% on top of May’s 4.6% gain. We estimate non-tech exports volume fell 0.3% for the second monthly decline after April’s robust rise. In the three-month trend, tech exports volume growth rebounded to 13.5%3m/3m, saar from -7.5% in April, and non-tech exports volume growth also recovered to 10.6%.

Comparing the nominal vs. real exports growth in 2Q, headline figures suggest that the overall nominal exports growth was mostly led by volume growth instead of price gains, as 2Q’s nominal exports growth was 13.3%3m/3m, saar (vs. 11.4% volume growth). That said, both price and volume growth was strong in the tech sector (nominal growth was 43.0%3m/3m, saar and volume growth was 13.5%, Figure 4), while non-tech exports prices were a drag on nominal exports growth (non-tech nominal growth was 2.1% compared with 10.6% volume growth).

Figure 4: Tech exports volume vs. value
![](images/a12f6c3a09f4535c84790120cc94739bb00302504f34508b4b15fc3549527f66.jpg)
Source: MoTIE, BoK, and J.P. Morgan

## ASEAN

• Singapore’s advance 2Q24 GDP depicts multi-speed recovery

• Goods production remains on an uplift while private consumption has been softening

Core CPI should remain on a downward trajectory led by goods while services should also slow as ULC eases

We expect that the MAS will be on hold through 2024 as core CPI reverts to around 2%oya by end-year

Singapore’s advance 2Q24 GDP—which primarily incorporates the first two months of supply side data—expanded 2.9%oya and was up 1.6%q/q, saar, marking an ongoing deceleration from 2H23, which on average expanded 4.5%q/ q, saar. All told, the data depict a multi-speed recovery, with expansion in the goods sector offset by slowing services, and broadly speaks to a plateauing in domestic activity.

Although we expect that domestic demand could remain on the soft side, the impact on pricing behavior is less clear given the residual impact of pent-up price pressures which we expect will see a diminishing role in 2H24. This then leaves the marginal drivers of core CPI from imported goods prices—which we proxy via the DSPI (domestic supply price index)—and a markup, reflected in labor costs, among other inputs. On this latter point, unit labor cost have reverted to the prior historical trend and we expect core CPI should too, which in our view would keep the MAS on hold though this year.

## A multi-speed recovery

Although the headline GDP expansion was broadly as expected, the details reveal a bifurcation between the goods and non-goods producing sectors, with goods expanding 2%q/q, saar following a 16.5% contraction in 1Q24 while the services producing sector was flat on the quarter following a strong 7.7%q/q, saar expansion in 1Q24 (Figures 1 and 2).

Figure 1: Singapore GDP index level
![](images/bc0ca10859704f425c3bafe5f3c7ba1fb0846abd4526618aa5076e5878ba0ec8.jpg)

Figure 2: Singapore GDP
![](images/d260f541150a07c064481cc7a3c6c7a79cb1462ca287b8f6301d471df8e81c31.jpg)
The relative softness in domestic demand, particularly private consumption, is evident in the monthly data, with both real retail sales and food and beverage spending respectively plateauing and softening this year (Figures 3 and 4).

Figure 3: Singapore retail sales ex. motor vehicles Index, 2017=100, sa, real terms
![](images/02dabcf3ce560575a3afa12226345f80bb7ff912f58544dcfcf032bc4dbf15a9.jpg)

Figure 4: Singapore food and beverage sales Index, 2017=100, sa, real terms
![](images/8898939affaa92ede504162edba9174a2fc9d2b236b3d4d99866e8d382374d9e.jpg)
These developments are broadly consistent with our forecast view of soft domestic demand, and affirm our view that core CPI should slow in sequential terms over the course of this year. The May core CPI reading is consistent with this view.

## Core CPI on path to slow further

Singapore’s May core CPI (which excludes private road transport and accommodation) was up 0.2%m/m, sa and came in line with expectations at 3.1%oya (J.P. Morgan: 3.1%oya, Consensus: 3.1%oya). In trend sequential terms, core CPI rose 1.9%3m/3m, saar and we expect the sequential trend for core CPI to continue easing over 2H24 (Figure 5).

Figure 5 Singapore core inflation
![](images/d8e5c5c2fc3e7aff7971fb1fb1784111276aa0d060da98640a74f9e139cfa1c3.jpg)
Source: DoS, J.P. Morgan forecasts in shaded area

There are two components in core CPI we believe will underpin this view and are evident in the recent bifurcation in food prices, which illustrates the pricing dynamics in Singapore between goods and services (Figure 6). Although food prices have turned down, food service prices have remained elevated vis-à-vis the pre-pandemic run rates, reflecting the residual impact of pipeline price pressures.

Figure 6: Singapore food prices %3m, saar
![](images/1e0ae98bd1ecf363bc96b3cb1af2deb8c57425e9031b5459fea4773b3857998b.jpg)

We expect core goods prices to remain stable, in line with the DSPI (Domestic Supply Price Index), which tends to lead by around 4 months. The recent rise in core goods CPI relative to the DSPI in 1Q23 and 1Q24 likely owes to the impact of the 1%-pt rise in the Goods and Services Tax (GST) and we expect a reversion to the historical trend in 2H24 (Figure 7).

Figure 7: Singapore DSPI and core goods CPI %3m/3m, saar
![](images/1fd9c7de6f737f645d5c334b3a7d297e957f9d71ba3a3dde0bfefd948a599924.jpg)

Figure 8: Singapore unit labor cost in services
![](images/bb4419198c43f625cf5283382fe6cea3f1d59a916d0d3420c51e3d0101d5cf79.jpg)

## Core services in focus

On core service inflation, the correlation between unit labor costs and core services appears to be less robust but with ULC expected to continue on its benign path, we also expect that core services inflation to ease (Figure 8). In this context, although ULC has slowed, this owes mainly to productivity gains that have offset the recent rise in payrolls. However, given the somewhat higher labor input in high-touch service facing industries—including food and beverage— it remains to be seen whether broader productivity gains might be able to mitigate the rise in labor costs in these sectors (Figure 9).

Figure 9: Singapore core services ex travel and unit labor cost %3m/3m, saar
![](images/9b45b75f9cb1b91658321b520926c937f957e2eccce685e626340e18034deac9.jpg)

## India

• Trade deficit narrows as oil imports normalise

• Service imports hit an all-time high in June

• Higher food and core prices drive WPI inflation to a 16-month high

The merchandise trade deficit narrowed in June on account of a normalisation of oil imports, but the non-oil trade deficit widened to its highest level in seven months. On their part, net service exports remained steady even as imports reached a record high in June.

Meanwhile WPI inflation continues to firm printing at 3.4% oya — the highest in 16 months. This was underpinned by both firming food and core prices, the latter reflecting higher base metal prices. However, large favourable base effects are expected to depress year-on-year WPI inflation in 3Q.

## Trade deficit narrows vs May

The merchandise trade deficit narrowed to \$21 bn in June from \$23.8 bn (nsa) in May but continued to be higher than the average of the previous 6 months (Figure 1). The narrowing was on the back of a normalisation of oil imports to \$15.1 bn after they surged to \$19.9 bn in May (Figure 2). Ex oil, however, the trade deficit widened to its highest level in 7 months. Manufacturing exports as well as imports—a proxy for volume—fell sequentially in June on the back of strong sequential growth in May.

Figure 1: Merchandise trade deficit
![](images/08bbc0ad7e1a9fb72b814ccd3087db535f0fbb130f5f9e1bf6185857ed3e39f4.jpg)

Figure 2: Oil imports
![](images/69e309671f0a8796a030f7750272769c4944052edaf4ed5c7137d56a55142ba0.jpg)

## Service imports hit an all-time high

Net service exports remained steady at \$13 bn in June, with gross exports slightly increasing to \$30.3 bn from \$29.8 bn in May. Service imports reached a record \$17.3 bn, likely driven by an increase in international travel by Indians (Figure 3). With the service balance remaining steady, the decrease of the merchandise trade deficit resulted in a fall in the combined (merchandise and services) deficit to \$8 bn, from \$10.8 bn in May (Figure 4). The trends so far this quarter are likely to result in a swing in the current account balance (CAB) from a minor surplus in 1Q24 to a deficit of \~1.5% of GDP in 2Q24.

Figure 4: Combined (merchandise and services) trade deficit \$ bn, 3mma, nsa
![](images/13a58f3c617d820c796ff6960d01cd52f61cae45415298fd18469bb0ca850f17.jpg)

## WPI inflation continues to firm

Wholesale Price Index (WPI) inflation printed 3.4% oya in June, picking up from 2.6% oya in May. Sequentially, it gained 0.2% after a monthly gain of 0.6% in May (Figure 5). This was largely driven by stronger wholesale food prices (+1.2% m/m sa) with significant increases in vegetables, fruits, and pulses. That said, core prices also strengthened reflecting a surge in base metal prices. Sequentially, core firmed up by 0.5% m/m, sa in June, similar to May’s increase. Looking ahead, WPI inflation is expected to ease again in 3Q24 on a year-on-year basis due to large and favourable base effects. However, a higher WPI print in 2Q vis-a-vis 1Q should boost the GDP deflator, which has been very low recently.

Figure 5: Wholesale price index (WPI)
![](images/0443c9ab145fcc922c26c5efcbf2d6aec29ca83f371f694ee6a381ecc3c56681.jpg)

## Asia Focus: Export prices

The region’s export price deflation has been an important contributor to global inflation moderation in the past two years. However, the impulse seems to have faded. A breakdown of US import prices by origin shows that yoy deflation narrowed steadily in 1H24 (Figure 1). Mainland China’s export prices declined 3.2%oya in RMB terms or 6.5% in USD terms in May (vs. -8.8% and -13.6% in January). The %3m run-rate has returned to modest gains since the start of this year, although lingering domestic PPI deflation suggests that export price deflation may still have legs (Figure 2).

By product, the moderation in export price deflation is broadbased in China, most evident in machinery and transportation equipment (Figure 3). It is similar to Japan, as its transportation products’ export prices on a contract-currency basis turned positive in mid-2023, followed by a steady upturn from June (Figure 4). Tech-related products stand out. In Korea, electronic, optical equipment and electrical equipment have been key drivers for headline export prices, taking on a more dominant role in 2Q24 and contributing around 80% to the gain in the headline print (Figure 5). In Taiwan, after staying in deflationary territory for nearly two years, export prices for tech-related products turned positive in May, and may become more of a lift to export prices ahead (Figure 6).

## Explanation of Emerging Markets Sovereign Research Ratings System and Valuation & Methodology:

Ratings System: J.P. Morgan uses the following issuer portfolio weightings for Emerging Markets Sovereign Research: Overweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to outperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns); Marketweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to perform in line with the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns); and Underweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to underperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns). NR is Not Rated. In this case, J.P. Morgan has removed the rating for this security because of either legal, regulatory or policy reasons or because of lack of a sufficient fundamental basis. The previous rating no longer should be relied upon. An NR designation is not a recommendation or a rating. NC is Not Covered. An NC designation is not a rating or a recommendation. Recommendations will be at the issuer level, and an issuer recommendation applies to all of the index-eligible bonds at the same level for the issuer. When we change the issuer-level rating, we are changing the rating for all of the issues covered, unless otherwise specified. Ratings for quasi-sovereign issuers in the EMBIG may differ from the ratings provided in EM corporate coverage.

Valuation & Methodology: For J.P. Morgan's Emerging Markets Sovereign Research, we assign a rating to each sovereign issuer (Overweight, Marketweight or Underweight) based on our view of whether the combination of the issuer’s fundamentals, market technicals, and the relative value of its securities will cause it to outperform, perform in line with, or underperform the credit returns of the EMBIGD index over the next three months. Our view of an issuer’s fundamentals includes our opinion of whether the issuer is becoming more or less able to service its deb obligations when they become due and payable, as well as whether its willingness to service debt obligations is increasing or decreasing.
